Analytical Fraud-Detection Techniques: Benford's Law and Journal Entry Testing

Analytical fraud-detection techniques
Core methodsDigit-frequency analysis (Benford's Law); risk-based journal entry testing
Key standardSAS No. 99 (2002; now AU-C 240)
Benford's Law sourceGorenc (2019)
Journal entry sourceLanza & Gilbert (2007)
Highest-risk entry type"Top-side" entries bypassing subledgers
Internal controls rank4th among detection methods (ACFE)
Leading detection methodTips

Analytical fraud-detection techniques are quantitative screening methods that examine the numbers themselves, rather than physical documents or witness testimony, to identify transactions or financial statements warranting closer scrutiny. Two techniques dominate the practitioner literature: digit-frequency analysis under Benford's Law, and risk-based journal entry testing, a structured search of the general ledger developed in response to auditing standards that followed the Enron-era accounting scandals. Both techniques share a common epistemic limitation that runs through this article: neither one proves fraud on its own. Each generates a lead — a stratum of numbers or a cluster of journal entries statistically or structurally unusual enough to merit investigation — that must then be corroborated against source documents, interviews, and the broader constellation of red flags before any conclusion is drawn. This article expands on the mechanics of Benford's Law covered in its own dedicated article by situating the technique within the academic literature on its evidentiary limits, and introduces the parallel discipline of journal entry testing, including its "5 Ws" methodology and its central finding about where financial statement fraud is most likely to hide.

1 Benford's Law as a screening tool, not proof

The full mechanics of digit-frequency analysis — the expected first-digit distribution, its history, and the data conditions required for a valid test — are covered in the dedicated article on Benford's Law. This section focuses narrowly on how the technique is framed as legal evidence, drawing on Gorenc's 2019 study "Benford's Law As a Useful Tool to Determine Fraud in Financial Statements," published in Management: Journal of Contemporary Management Issues.[1]

Gorenc's central and most consequential claim is a limiting one: a departure from the expected Benford distribution in a set of reported financial figures is insufficient, standing alone, to prove fraud in a court of law.[1] The statistical test can establish that a dataset's leading-digit profile deviates from what an unconstrained, naturally occurring set of numbers should look like, but it cannot by itself identify who altered the numbers, why, or even whether the deviation reflects intentional manipulation as opposed to a benign structural feature of the data (assigned identifiers, price points, an imposed threshold, or a genuinely unusual but honest business event). This is precisely the same caution developed at length in the Benford's Law article's discussion of requirements and limitations, but Gorenc's framing sharpens it into an explicit evidentiary boundary: the test is a screening and prioritization tool that tells an examiner where to look, not a finding of fact that tells a court what happened.

This distinction matters practically because it defines the correct role of digit analysis inside an investigation. A Benford deviation in, say, a population of vendor disbursements does not itself constitute an allegation; it identifies a stratum of transactions for the examiner to pull and trace to source documents — purchase orders, contracts, receiving reports, and correspondence — using the fuller toolkit of forensic accounting. Only that corroborating evidence, not the statistical anomaly itself, can support a legal conclusion that fraud occurred.

2 Linking digit analysis to fraud theory

Gorenc situates Benford's Law within the same theoretical scaffolding used throughout fraud examination: Cressey's fraud triangle (pressure, opportunity, rationalization) and the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners' three-branch fraud tree taxonomy of corruption, asset misappropriation, and financial statement fraud.[1] The connection to the fraud triangle is direct: digit-frequency analysis is aimed at surfacing the physical trace left by the opportunity leg once it has been exploited. A manager under pressure to hit an earnings target, and rationalizing the act as temporary or victimless, still has to produce a set of numbers that will pass casual review. Because fabricated figures tend to distribute leading digits more evenly than genuine ones, or cluster suspiciously around round numbers and psychological thresholds, the digit pattern is one of the few artifacts of the underlying human decision that survives into the dataset itself and can be tested independently of the perpetrator's own account.

Within the ACFE's fraud tree, Benford's Law is most useful against financial statement fraud specifically, because that branch produces large populations of unconstrained financial figures — account balances, journal-entry amounts, revenue line items — of exactly the kind the test requires. It is markedly less useful against classic asset misappropriation schemes involving a small number of large, individually fabricated transactions, since a handful of fictitious entries buried in an otherwise honest population will rarely shift the aggregate digit distribution enough to register statistically. This is one reason digit analysis functions best as a complement to, rather than a substitute for, targeted testing of specific transaction populations such as journal entries.

3 Risk-based journal entry testing

Where Benford's Law screens an entire population of figures for statistical anomalies, journal entry testing is a more targeted discipline aimed directly at the general ledger. Lanza and Gilbert's 2007 Journal of Accountancy article "A Risk-Based Approach to Journal Entry Testing" lays out the methodology that has become the standard practitioner reference.[2] The article's premise is that journal entry testing is not optional or merely good practice: it is a requirement directly responsive to SAS No. 99, Consideration of Fraud in a Financial Statement Audit (2002, now codified as AU-C 240), the same post-Enron standard discussed in the Red Flags and Fraud Triangle articles.[3] SAS 99 obligates auditors to test the appropriateness of journal entries recorded in the general ledger and other adjustments made in preparing financial statements, specifically because management override of controls, executed through journal entries, is recognized as one of the primary mechanisms of financial statement fraud.

Lanza and Gilbert's methodology departs from traditional sampling in favor of examining the full population of journal entries rather than a statistical subset, using data-analysis software such as Excel, ACL, or IDEA to filter, stratify, and rank entries by risk characteristics.[2] This full-population approach is possible precisely because journal entry data is structured and extractable in a way that, for example, informal conversations or physical documents are not, making it well suited to the same category of analytical methods that includes digit-frequency testing. The goal is not to review every entry with equal scrutiny but to use risk criteria to triage the population down to a manageable, high-yield subset for manual examination.

4 The "5 Ws" methodology

Lanza and Gilbert organize their risk criteria around a journalistic mnemonic borrowed from investigative reporting: the "5 Ws" — Who, What, When, Where, and Why.[2] Each question targets a different dimension of a journal entry that can reveal risk:

QuestionWhat it testsExample risk indicator
WhoThe identity and authorization of the preparer and approverEntries prepared or posted by individuals outside their normal role or authority; entries lacking a second, independent approver
WhatThe nature, size, and account classification of the entryUnusually large manual entries; entries affecting sensitive accounts such as revenue, reserves, or related-party balances; entries stratified and ranked by dollar size
WhenThe timing of the postingEntries posted on weekends, holidays, or outside normal business hours; entries concentrated at period-end or year-end close, when management has the strongest incentive and the least time for review
WhereThe account or ledger location of the entryPostings to dormant, suspense, or rarely used accounts; entries routed through unusual account combinations
WhyThe business rationale and threshold behavior of the entryRound-dollar amounts (a common signature of an estimated or fabricated figure rather than a computed one); amounts falling just under an approval or reporting threshold; entries lacking adequate supporting documentation or a clear business purpose

These five questions function as a structured extension of the same reasoning found in the Red Flags article's accounting-record category, translated into extractable, filterable data criteria that can be run programmatically across a full general-ledger population rather than relied upon as a manual checklist. In practice, an examiner applying this framework filters the ledger by each of the 5 Ws in combination — for example, isolating round-dollar entries (Why) posted by a small number of senior preparers (Who) at or near period-end (When) — to produce a short list of the highest-risk entries for individual tracing to supporting documentation.

5 Top-side entries and the subledger bypass problem

The single most important finding in Lanza and Gilbert's methodology concerns where fraudulent journal entries are most likely to originate. Most day-to-day accounting entries flow up from subledgers — accounts receivable, accounts payable, payroll, fixed assets — each governed by its own set of controls, reconciliations, and often multiple people involved in processing, which makes subledger-level manipulation comparatively difficult to execute and conceal without collusion.

"Top-side" journal entries, by contrast, are adjustments made directly at the corporate or consolidation level, entered straight into the general ledger or the consolidation software without passing through any subledger and without triggering the subledger's normal control checks.[2] Lanza and Gilbert identify top-side entries as the highest-risk vehicle for management override of internal controls, precisely because they bypass the layered, cross-departmental controls that would otherwise require multiple people to notice or participate in a manipulation. A single senior manager or a small executive-level team with consolidation access can post a top-side entry that overstates revenue, reduces expenses, or reclassifies a liability, without any subledger clerk, accounts-payable processor, or departmental reconciliation ever seeing or questioning the transaction. This is the general ledger's version of the same override risk discussed in the Financial Statement Fraud article: financial statement fraud is fundamentally a management crime, committed by the people positioned to override the very controls designed to catch ordinary employees, and top-side entries are the specific mechanical instrument through which that override is most often executed.

Testing routines built on this methodology accordingly weight the Who, Where, and When criteria of the 5 Ws toward isolating consolidation-level posting access, entries with no subledger source system, and postings clustered at the close calendar's most sensitive dates — the intersection where a top-side override is most likely to surface.

6 Why internal controls rank only fourth

Lanza and Gilbert ground the urgency of journal entry testing in a finding drawn from ACFE research: internal controls rank only fourth among the methods by which occupational frauds are actually detected.[2] This finding has been substantially reaffirmed in subsequent ACFE Report to the Nations editions, which consistently find that tips from employees, customers, vendors, or anonymous sources detect a larger share of frauds than any other single method, well ahead of internal audit, management review, or the control environment itself, and that over half of all frauds are attributable to a lack of internal controls or the override of controls that do exist.[4]

The implication is not that controls are worthless, but that a sophisticated employee, and especially a member of senior management with the authority to make top-side entries, can and routinely does circumvent standard control structures.[2] A control environment built to catch clerical errors or lower-level misappropriation is not designed to catch a person authorized to bypass it entirely. This is the rationale for a separate, proactive testing routine that does not rely on the control environment functioning correctly in the first place, but independently interrogates the very population of entries that environment was supposed to govern.

7 Red-flag perception research

A separate but related line of research asks not which techniques detect fraud most effectively, but which red flags practitioners themselves believe are most diagnostic. Moyes (2008), "CPAs' Perceptions of Red Flags Used in Detecting Fraud," published in The ICFAI Journal of Audit Practice, surveyed certified public accountants on the perceived effectiveness of fraud indicators, organizing the results along the same three categories as the fraud triangle: pressure, opportunity, and rationalization.[5] The full text of this specific study was not independently verified for this article; the finding below is reported as consistent with a broader Moyes research program on auditor red-flag perception and should be treated with appropriate caution rather than as a confirmed, independently checked result.

The pattern reported across this literature is that CPAs tend to rate red flags tied to management's attitude or rationalization — such as a known history of securities-law violations, or management's attempts to limit the scope of an audit — as more effective indicators of fraud than red flags tied to opportunity or to incentive and pressure alone.[5] If accurate, this has a direct practical implication for how an investigator or OSINT researcher triages limited time and resources: a documented pattern of management resistance to scrutiny, or a track record of prior misconduct, may be a more efficient early filter than combing for opportunity-side control weaknesses or pressure-side financial strain, both of which are common in the ordinary course of business and carry a much higher false-positive rate on their own. This finding should be read alongside the fuller discussion of red-flag categories, the SAS lineage, and the general caution that red flags raise questions rather than answer them, in the dedicated Red Flags article.

8 Combining the techniques in practice

Digit-frequency analysis and journal entry testing are complementary rather than competing techniques, and the strongest practical fraud-detection programs run both together as part of the broader suite of analytical methods. Benford's Law is best deployed as a wide, cheap, first-pass screen across an entire population of financial figures — disbursements, account balances, reported line items — to flag strata of numbers whose digit distribution warrants a closer look. Journal entry testing, by contrast, is a narrower, deeper, and more structurally targeted search of the general ledger specifically, built around the 5 Ws and weighted heavily toward the top-side entries identified as the highest-risk vehicle for management override. A well-designed program might use a Benford deviation in a class of accounts to help decide which subledgers or time periods deserve the most intensive journal entry testing, or use the "Why" criterion of round-dollar and threshold-adjacent amounts as a shared filter across both techniques.

Neither technique substitutes for the corroborating work of a full forensic accounting investigation: tracing flagged entries to source documentation, interviewing preparers and approvers, and testing the business rationale offered for each anomaly. Both are, in Gorenc's phrase, tools that determine where to look, not tools that determine what happened. No purely quantitative or purely control-based method has displaced the tip as the single most effective fraud-detection mechanism; the analytical techniques described here sharpen the search once suspicion has already opened the door to a deeper investigation.

See also: Benford's Law, Red Flags, Fraud Triangle, Financial Statement Fraud, Analytical Methods, Forensic Accounting.

Net Worth Method

Net Worth Method
TypeIndirect method of proof
Core identityAssets − Liabilities = Net worth
PurposeEstimate unreported / illegal income
OriginU.S. tax prosecutions (Al Capone, 1931)
RelatedSource-and-application-of-funds method

The net worth method is an indirect method of proof used by forensic accountants, tax investigators, and fraud examiners to estimate a subject's income when direct records of that income are unavailable, incomplete, or concealed. Rather than adding up income directly, the method infers it: if a person's wealth and spending grow by more than their known, legitimate income can account for, the unexplained increase is treated as unreported — and often illegal — income. It is a foundational technique in following the money and sits alongside the analytical methods used to build financial cases.[1]

1 Concept and formula

The method rests on the accounting identity that net worth is what a person owns less what they owe, measured at a point in time:

Assets − Liabilities = Net worth

The investigator constructs a net worth statement at the start and end of a period (typically a tax year) from all identifiable assets — cash, bank balances, real estate, vehicles, investments, business interests — and all liabilities. The increase in net worth over the period is then combined with the subject's living expenses and adjusted for known, legitimate income to isolate the unexplained portion:

Change in net worth
  + Living expenses (non-deductible personal outlays)
  − Known / reported income (and non-taxable receipts)
  = Unexplained (unreported or illegal) income

The logic is that every dollar a person acquires must either be spent, saved (increasing net worth), or used to pay down debt. If the sum of spending and net-worth growth exceeds documented income, the difference had to come from somewhere the subject did not report. A closing net worth of $600,000 against an opening $200,000, plus $150,000 of living expenses, against $180,000 of reported income implies roughly $370,000 of unexplained income for the period. The method is called indirect precisely because it never observes the illicit income directly; it deduces the income's existence and size from its footprints in assets and spending. That indirection is its great strength against subjects who keep no books for their illegal earnings, and also its principal vulnerability, since the conclusion is only as reliable as the completeness of the asset inventory and the accuracy of the starting point.

2 Origin: the Capone case

The net worth method is closely associated with the U.S. prosecution of gangster Al Capone, who was convicted of tax evasion in 1931. Investigators could not document Capone's illegal earnings directly, but they could demonstrate a lifestyle and accumulation of wealth far beyond any income he had declared, establishing that he must have had large unreported income. The approach proved that financial evidence could reach criminals whose underlying crimes were otherwise hard to charge, and it became a template for indirect proof in tax and financial-crime investigations. The U.S. Supreme Court later addressed the method's evidentiary use, requiring investigators to establish an accurate opening net worth ("starting point") and to investigate leads pointing to non-taxable sources of the increase.[2]

3 Use in tax and laundering cases

Indirect methods remain a staple of tax-fraud enforcement and of money-laundering investigations, where the whole purpose of laundering is to obscure the origin of funds and defeat direct tracing. When layered transfers and front businesses frustrate a transaction-by-transaction reconstruction, the net worth method attacks the problem from the outside: it measures the wealth that ultimately lands with the subject regardless of the path it took. It is frequently used to corroborate asset tracing, to support forfeiture of unexplained wealth, and to quantify the proceeds of predicate offenses. Because it relies on comprehensive financial records, it also draws on the same bank, wire, and public-record sources that underpin open-source financial investigation.[3]

4 Source-and-application-of-funds method

A closely related indirect technique is the source-and-application-of-funds method (also called the expenditures method). Instead of comparing net worth at two dates, it lists all applications of funds during the period — every expenditure and increase in assets or decrease in liabilities — and all sources of funds — income and other legitimate receipts, decreases in assets, increases in liabilities. If applications exceed sources, the excess represents funds from an undisclosed source:

Total applications (expenditures + asset increases + debt reductions)
  − Total known sources (reported income + asset decreases + new borrowing)
  = Funds from undisclosed source

The two methods are analytically equivalent and often computed together as a cross-check: the source-and-application approach is frequently easier when a subject spends heavily rather than accumulating assets, while the net worth statement is stronger when wealth is being stockpiled. Both are indirect methods of proof, and both must account for cash on hand, gifts, loans, and inheritances that could innocently explain the gap.

5 Requirements and challenges

Indirect methods are only as sound as their inputs, and defense challenges typically target three points. First, the opening net worth must be established accurately — an understated starting point manufactures phantom income in later years, which is why the "cash hoard" defense (claiming a large undocumented pile of cash existed at the start) is common. Second, investigators must pursue non-taxable and legitimate sources — loans, gifts, inheritances, prior savings, and asset sales — that could explain an increase without wrongdoing; failure to run down such leads undermines the case. Third, living expenses must be reasonably estimated rather than assumed. Well-documented net worth cases therefore lean heavily on primary records and, where necessary, on the subject's own admissions during interviews. Properly built, the method yields a defensible estimate of concealed income; carelessly built, it collapses under a plausible innocent explanation.

See also: Asset Tracing, Money Laundering, Analytical Methods.

References

  1. Crain, M., Hopwood, W., Pacini, C. & Young, G. Essentials of Forensic Accounting (AICPA), 2015. (indirect methods of proof)
  2. Holland v. United States, 348 U.S. 121 (1954); origin in the Al Capone prosecution, 1931. (evidentiary requirements for the net worth method; general knowledge / case law)
  3. OSCE. "Following the Money: Compendium of Resources and Good Practices on Financial Investigations Related to Trafficking in Human Beings." 2019.

ACFE Report to the Nations

See also: Fraud Tree | Occupational Fraud | Red Flags

Occupational Fraud 2024: A Report to the Nations
PublisherAssociation of Certified Fraud Examiners (ACFE)
Cases analyzed1,921
Countries/territories138
Industry categories22
Total losses (study sample)>$3.1 billion
Estimated annual revenue lost to fraud5%
Median loss per case$1.7 million
Leading detection methodTips (43%)
Publication cadenceBiennial

The ACFE Report to the Nations is the flagship biennial study of occupational fraud published by the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners (ACFE), and it is widely treated as the primary benchmark dataset in occupational-fraud research. Unlike self-report perception surveys or media-derived incident counts, the report is built directly from case data supplied by Certified Fraud Examiners (CFEs) who personally investigated each fraud, giving it a distinctive evidentiary grounding among the small handful of large-sample fraud studies published on a recurring schedule. This article summarizes the findings of the 2024 edition, Occupational Fraud 2024: A Report to the Nations,[1] and situates it against three comparable benchmark surveys published by large professional-services firms — PwC's Global Economic Crime and Fraud Survey, KPMG's Global Profiles of the Fraudster, and EY's Global Forensic Data Analytics Survey — while addressing a common misconception about which firms publish this kind of study at all.

1 Methodology and Scope

The ACFE's 2024 edition analyzed 1,921 real cases of occupational fraud investigated between January 2022 and September 2023, drawn from 138 countries/territories and classified across 22 industry categories.[1] "Occupational fraud" in ACFE usage refers specifically to fraud committed by an employee, manager, owner, or executive against the organization that employs them — as distinct from fraud committed by outsiders against the organization, or by the organization against outsiders (see Occupational Fraud for the full definitional discussion). Each case in the dataset was submitted by a CFE who directly investigated it, meaning the underlying data reflects confirmed, adjudicated, or internally substantiated fraud rather than suspected or self-reported incidents. This case-sourced methodology is the report's central methodological strength and its central limitation in the same breath: it produces unusually granular, verified case-level detail (loss amount, perpetrator characteristics, detection method, industry, scheme type) but is not a random sample of all fraud in the economy — it necessarily captures only fraud that was detected, investigated, and reported by a participating CFE, leaving undetected or unreported fraud outside the dataset by construction.

2 Headline Findings

The 2024 study's top-line statistics are the numbers most frequently cited across fraud-examination training, corporate compliance materials, and OSINT/forensic-accounting literature:

  • Global occupational fraud losses across the study sample exceeded $3.1 billion.[1]
  • Organizations are estimated to lose approximately 5% of annual revenue to fraud.[1]
  • The median loss per case was approximately $1.7 million.[1]

These figures are typically read alongside the report's breakdown by fraud category (see below), because the aggregate median loss obscures a sharp divergence between how often a scheme type occurs and how costly it is when it does.

3 Fraud Type: Frequency vs. Median Loss

The ACFE organizes occupational fraud into three categories under its long-standing Fraud Tree taxonomy: asset misappropriation, corruption, and financial statement fraud. The 2024 data shows an inverse relationship between how common each category is and how much it typically costs:

Fraud categoryShare of casesMedian loss
Asset misappropriation89%$120,000
Corruption48%$200,000
Financial statement fraud~5%$766,000

(Percentages sum to more than 100% because a single case can involve more than one category.) Asset misappropriation — theft or misuse of an organization's resources, such as skimming, check tampering, or expense reimbursement fraud — is by far the most frequent scheme type, occurring in 89% of cases, but it also carries the lowest median loss of the three categories. Corruption schemes, including bribery, conflicts of interest, and illegal gratuities, appear in roughly half of cases with a moderately higher median loss. Financial statement fraud — the deliberate misstatement of an organization's financial reports, as seen in cases such as Enron and WorldCom — is the rarest category by far, appearing in only about 5% of cases, but is by a wide margin the most financially damaging, with a median loss more than six times that of asset misappropriation. This frequency/severity inversion is one of the report's most cited findings, because it implies that the schemes an organization is statistically most likely to encounter (asset misappropriation) are not the ones most likely to threaten its survival (financial statement fraud).

4 Detection Methods and the Role of Tips

Among all detection methods tracked by the ACFE, tips were the leading method by a wide margin, accounting for 43% of frauds detected — more than three times the rate of the next most common detection method.[1] Tips typically originate from employees, but also from customers, vendors, competitors, and anonymous sources, frequently via a formal whistleblower hotline or reporting mechanism. This finding is consistent across multiple cycles of the ACFE study and is echoed independently in KPMG's fraudster-profile research (see below), which also finds whistleblower tips to be the single most effective detection mechanism, ahead of both internal and external audit. The persistence of this finding across independently conducted surveys is treated in the fraud-examination literature as strong evidence that formal audit procedures and technology-driven controls, while valuable, are not the primary way most real-world fraud actually comes to light — a theme directly relevant to the design of red flag monitoring programs and whistleblower policy.

5 Internal Controls Failure

The 2024 report also found that over half of frauds in the dataset were attributable to either a lack of internal controls or the override of internal controls that did exist.[1] This finding reinforces a recurring theme across the fraud-examination and forensic-accounting literature: management override of controls is a persistent vulnerability precisely because senior employees with the authority to bypass ordinary safeguards are also the employees best positioned to conceal fraud from routine detection. The finding is consistent with material discussed in Occupational Fraud regarding the role of the fraud triangle's "opportunity" element, and with journal-entry-testing methodology developed specifically to detect control overrides at the point of highest risk — top-side, consolidation-level entries that bypass normal subledger processing.

6 Comparable Surveys: PwC, KPMG, and EY

The ACFE's Report to the Nations is the most granular case-level fraud study published on a recurring cycle, but it is not the only large-sample fraud benchmark survey. Three major professional-services firms publish comparable recurring studies, though each uses a materially different methodology, sample, and framing than the ACFE's case-sourced approach.

6.1 PwC — Global Economic Crime and Fraud Survey

PwC's Global Economic Crime and Fraud Survey (GECS) has run biennially for over 20 years and, unlike the ACFE's CFE-sourced case data, is an executive perception survey.[2] The 2022 edition surveyed 1,296 executives across 53 countries and regions: 46% of participating organizations reported experiencing fraud, corruption, or other economic crime in the preceding 24 months, with total reported losses of roughly $42 billion across the sample. Cybercrime, customer fraud, and asset misappropriation were the most commonly reported crime types, and nearly 70% of fraud-experiencing organizations cited external or combined external-and-internal collusion as the most disruptive source of loss. The 2024 edition shifted emphasis toward cybercrime specifically, reporting it as both the most prevalent (44% incident rate) and most disruptive (40%) form of economic crime — reflecting the continued digitization of fraud discussed more broadly in the wiki's digital-forensics material.

6.2 KPMG — Global Profiles of the Fraudster

KPMG's Global Profiles of the Fraudster series takes a different angle than either the ACFE or PwC studies: rather than measuring loss totals or incident rates, it analyzes hundreds of actual fraud investigations conducted by KPMG member firms to build a statistical and behavioral profile of the typical occupational fraud perpetrator.[3] Recent editions find the "typical" fraudster is male (in over 80% of cases), aged roughly 36 to 55, acting against his own employer, and holding a senior management or executive position with more than six years of tenure. Roughly 55% of cases involve collusion among two to five people — a materially higher collusion rate than a purely opportunistic, single-actor model of occupational fraud would predict. The most common fraud type identified is misappropriation of assets, occurring in approximately 52% of cases, broadly consistent with the ACFE's own finding that asset misappropriation is the most frequent scheme category. Primary motivation is financial gain or greed, with opportunism arising from weak controls as a secondary driver. As noted above, KPMG's research independently corroborates the ACFE's tip-detection finding: whistleblowers and tip-offs are identified as the single most effective detection mechanism, ahead of both internal and external audit.

6.3 EY — Global Forensic Data Analytics Survey

EY's Global Forensic Data Analytics Survey is narrower in focus than the other three studies: rather than measuring fraud incidence or perpetrator profiles directly, it examines how organizations use forensic data analytics (FDA) — including robotic process automation and artificial intelligence — to detect and manage fraud, bribery, and corruption risk.[4] The survey has run biennially since 2014. Its 2018 edition, based on 745 executives across 19 countries, found 78% of respondents increasingly concerned about data protection and privacy compliance in connection with analytics deployment, 39% likely to adopt robotic process automation within a year, and 38% planning to adopt AI for compliance or forensic purposes. Respondents were broadly optimistic about analytics' investigative value — 88% and 87% cited improved risk assessment and improved detection within large datasets, respectively, as top perceived benefits. However, the survey's most frequently cited finding is a persistent capability gap: only 12–13% of respondents felt their organization had the right in-house forensic-data-analytics skills mix. This skills-gap finding is directly relevant to OSINT and forensic-accounting training, since it indicates that the limiting factor in modern fraud detection is often analyst capability and headcount rather than the availability of analytics tools themselves.

7 A Common Misconception: Deloitte and McKinsey

Because PwC, KPMG, and EY each publish a recurring, uniformly branded flagship fraud or forensic-analytics survey, it is a common assumption that the remaining two "Big Four"-adjacent firms — Deloitte and the consultancy McKinsey & Company — publish an equivalent single unified series. This is not accurate, and the distinction matters for anyone citing these firms' research.

Deloitte does not publish one unified "Global Forensic/Fraud Survey" comparable to PwC's GECS. Instead, Deloitte publishes multiple separately branded, regionally scoped surveys — for example, a Middle East Fraud Survey (2021 edition), India Corporate Fraud Perception Survey editions, and a CIS-region "Countering Corporate Fraud" survey — alongside general service-description material rather than one recurring flagship study with a consistent global sample and methodology. Common themes recur across these regional surveys nonetheless: rising fraud risk during and after the COVID-19 pandemic, a substantial share of organizations (41% in the Middle East survey) lacking a dedicated fraud risk management function, and increasing adoption of proactive forensic data analytics.

McKinsey similarly does not run a single recurring, uniformly branded fraud or risk report series. Its Risk & Resilience practice instead periodically publishes standalone insight articles on financial crime topics — for instance, "McKinsey on Risk, Issue 8" (Winter 2019, covering financial crime and anti-money-laundering), "Financial crime and fraud in the age of cybersecurity" (2019), and "A new approach to fighting fraud while enhancing customer experience" (November 2022) — rather than a single benchmark survey with a consistent methodology repeated on a fixed cadence.

The practical implication for an investigator or researcher citing fraud-benchmark statistics: PwC, KPMG, EY, and the ACFE can each be cited as a named, recurring flagship survey with a defined edition year and sample; "a Deloitte fraud survey" or "a McKinsey fraud report" should instead be cited as the specific regional or standalone publication in question, since no single "Deloitte Global Fraud Survey" or "McKinsey Fraud Report" series exists to point to.

8 Why the Benchmarks Diverge

Readers comparing figures across the ACFE, PwC, KPMG, and EY studies should note that the four surveys are not measuring the same thing, and their numbers should not be treated as directly interchangeable:

  • The ACFE report is built from case-level data supplied by CFEs who personally investigated each confirmed fraud — a bottom-up, investigator-sourced dataset of substantiated occupational fraud only.
  • PwC's GECS is a top-down executive perception survey capturing self-reported experience of economic crime broadly, including crimes committed by outsiders against the organization, not only occupational fraud by employees.
  • KPMG's fraudster-profile research is drawn from KPMG's own case files and is oriented toward perpetrator demographics and behavior rather than aggregate loss statistics.
  • EY's FDA survey measures organizational analytics adoption and capability, not fraud incidence or loss magnitude at all.

Despite these methodological differences, several findings recur independently across multiple surveys — most notably the primacy of tips/whistleblowers as a detection mechanism (ACFE and KPMG) and the centrality of internal-controls weaknesses to fraud opportunity (ACFE, KPMG, and Deloitte's regional surveys). Findings that appear consistently across independently constructed samples and methodologies are generally treated as more robust than any single survey's result in isolation.

9 Relevance to Investigation Practice

For a fraud examiner or OSINT investigator, the ACFE Report to the Nations functions as a baseline expectation-setting tool: it indicates which fraud category is statistically most likely to be encountered (asset misappropriation), which category poses the greatest financial risk if present (financial statement fraud), which detection channel is most likely to actually surface a scheme (tips), and which structural weakness most often enables fraud in the first place (control absence or override). These findings inform the practical guidance discussed in Red Flags regarding which behavioral and documentary indicators merit the highest investigative priority, and they connect directly to the taxonomy set out in the Fraud Tree article, which the ACFE study uses as its own organizing classification. Because the report is republished biennially with a fresh case sample, it is also used as a longitudinal benchmark to track whether detection methods, loss magnitudes, and scheme-type distributions are shifting over time — for example, the increasing role of digital and cyber-enabled fraud reflected in PwC's more recent editions.

References

  1. Association of Certified Fraud Examiners (ACFE). "Occupational Fraud 2024: A Report to the Nations." 2024. acfe.com
  2. PricewaterhouseCoopers International Limited (PwC). "Global Economic Crime and Fraud Survey." 2022. pwc.com
  3. KPMG International. "Global Profiles of the Fraudster." kpmg.com
  4. EY (Ernst & Young Global Limited). "Global Forensic Data Analytics Survey." 2018. icfp.co.za

Professional Ethics Standards for Forensic Accountants

Professional ethics standards for forensic accountants are the body of codified rules, principles, and standards issued by accounting and fraud-examination professional bodies that govern how practitioners conduct investigations, form opinions, and testify. Because forensic accountants routinely operate at the boundary between accounting analysis and legal proceedings, their professional obligations are drawn from several overlapping sources: the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants' (AICPA) Code of Professional Conduct and its consulting and valuation standards; the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners' (ACFE) Code of Professional Ethics; the International Standards on Auditing (ISA) issued by the International Auditing and Assurance Standards Board (IAASB), particularly ISA 240 governing an auditor's responsibilities relating to fraud; and a set of AICPA risk-assessment auditing standards issued in 2006. Together these frameworks define competence requirements, independence and objectivity rules, the limits of permissible opinion testimony, confidentiality and disclosure obligations, and the professional skepticism expected when fraud is suspected. A recurring theme across all of these codes is the deliberate separation of investigative fact-finding from legal adjudication: forensic accountants and fraud examiners are repeatedly instructed to determine what happened financially, not who is guilty.

Key Ethics Instruments
AICPA Code of Professional Conduct6 principles; governs CPAs generally
SSCS No. 11991; consulting/litigation-support engagements
SSVS No. 12007; effective engagements accepted on/after Jan. 1, 2008; valuation conclusions
ACFE Code of Professional Ethics8 canons; binds Certified Fraud Examiners
ISA 240IAASB; auditor's responsibilities relating to fraud
AICPA Risk Assessment Standards8 of 10 SASs; effective for periods beginning after Dec. 15, 2006

See also: Evidence and Testimony, Report Writing, Daubert Standard.

1 AICPA Code of Professional Conduct

The AICPA Code of Professional Conduct is the foundational ethics code binding members of the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants, including those practicing as forensic accountants or serving as expert witnesses. The Code is organized as a Preface followed by three Parts — Members in Public Practice, Members in Business, and Other Members — and is built around six overarching Principles of Professional Conduct:[1]

  • Responsibilities — members should exercise sensitive professional and moral judgment in all their activities.
  • The Public Interest — members should act to honor the public trust and demonstrate commitment to professionalism.
  • Integrity — members should perform all professional responsibilities with the highest sense of integrity.
  • Objectivity and Independence — members should maintain objectivity and be free of conflicts of interest, and members in public practice should be independent in fact and appearance when providing auditing and other attestation services.
  • Due Care — members should observe the profession's technical and ethical standards, strive to improve competence and quality of services, and discharge professional responsibility to the best of their ability.
  • Scope and Nature of Services — a member in public practice should observe these Principles in determining the scope and nature of services to provide.

For forensic accounting practice specifically, the Objectivity and Independence principle is the most operationally significant: it determines when a CPA can ethically accept and continue a forensic engagement, including litigation support and expert-witness work, without a disqualifying conflict of interest. Independence issues arise frequently in fraud investigations because the forensic accountant may have prior relationships with the client organization, its officers, or opposing parties, and the Code requires that such relationships be evaluated before and throughout an engagement. The Code's due-care principle also underlies the competence and evidence-sufficiency requirements that recur throughout forensic reporting and expert testimony.[1]

2 SSCS No. 1 — Consulting Services Standards

Most forensic accounting engagements — including fraud investigations and litigation support — are not audits, and are instead governed as consulting engagements under the Statement on Standards for Consulting Services No. 1 (SSCS No. 1), issued by the AICPA's Management Consulting Services Executive Committee in 1991 and now codified as CS Section 100.[2] SSCS No. 1 establishes two tiers of obligation for a practitioner performing consulting services:

  • General standards, applicable to all AICPA professional services: professional competence, due professional care, adequate planning and supervision, and obtaining sufficient relevant data to afford a reasonable basis for conclusions or recommendations.
  • Consulting-services-specific standards: serving the client's interest while complying with applicable professional standards; establishing a written or oral understanding with the client about the engagement's objectives, scope, and limitations; and informing the client of significant reservations, conflicts of interest, or limitations concerning the engagement's anticipated benefits.

Because forensic accounting engagements typically fall under this consulting framework rather than the attestation framework governing statutory audits, SSCS No. 1 is the standard most directly analogous to a forensic accountant's day-to-day duty of care — requiring the same evidentiary sufficiency threshold that later becomes relevant when an engagement's work product is challenged under evidentiary rules such as the Daubert standard for expert reliability.

3 SSVS No. 1 — Valuation Services Standards

Where a forensic accounting engagement culminates in a conclusion of value — as in damages calculations, shareholder disputes, marital dissolution, or fraud-loss quantification — the applicable standard is the Statement on Standards for Valuation Services No. 1 (SSVS No. 1), issued by the AICPA in June 2007 and effective for engagements accepted on or after January 1, 2008. It is now codified as VS Section 100.[3] SSVS No. 1 was the AICPA's first mandatory valuation standard and prescribes the procedures a member must follow when performing an engagement to estimate value, including:

  • determining the appropriate type of engagement (a formal valuation engagement or a more limited calculation engagement);
  • developing an understanding of the subject entity and the purpose and intended use of the valuation;
  • selecting and applying appropriate valuation approaches and methods; and
  • documenting and reporting the basis for the conclusion of value.

Because valuation conclusions produced under SSVS No. 1 are frequently the subject of admissibility challenges in litigation, the standard's documentation and methodology requirements are closely scrutinized in Daubert-type reliability challenges to expert valuation testimony — a court assessing whether a damages or valuation opinion rests on a reliable methodology will often look directly at whether SSVS No. 1's procedural requirements were followed.[3]

4 ACFE Code of Professional Ethics

The Association of Certified Fraud Examiners' Code of Professional Ethics is a concise, one-page code that every Certified Fraud Examiner (CFE) and ACFE member is required to abide by. It is organized into eight canons.[4] Three of these canons carry particular weight for how a fraud examination is conducted and reported:

  • Canon V requires an examiner to obtain evidence or other documentation to establish a reasonable basis for any opinion rendered, and — critically — to express no opinion regarding the guilt or innocence of any person or party. This canon codifies the central professional boundary of fraud examination: an examiner's role is to establish facts and support conclusions about what occurred financially, not to adjudicate criminal or civil liability, a determination reserved for courts, juries, and triers of fact. This principle mirrors the boundary discussed in Evidence and Testimony, where an expert witness's function is distinguished from that of a factfinder.
  • Canon VI establishes the examiner's confidentiality obligations, requiring that information obtained during an engagement not be disclosed except as authorized or legally required — a duty that governs how forensic accountants handle sensitive client and third-party financial data throughout an investigation.
  • Canon VII requires disclosure of all material matters discovered during an examination which, if omitted, could cause a distortion of the facts. Read together, Canons VI and VII strike a balance: examiners must protect confidential information from improper disclosure, while ensuring that findings material to the matter under examination are not selectively withheld in a way that would mislead the reader of a report.

The remaining canons address matters such as competence, diligence, and lawful conduct, but Canons V through VII are the ones most frequently invoked in disputes over the scope and content of a fraud examiner's report, and they directly inform the drafting conventions covered in Report Writing — particularly the practice of stating findings in terms of financial facts and patterns rather than legal conclusions.

5 ISA 240 and the Auditor's Fraud Responsibilities

The International Auditing and Assurance Standards Board (IAASB), established in 1978 under the International Federation of Accountants (IFAC) and reorganized in 2023 under a new International Foundation for Ethics and Audit to strengthen its independence, issues the International Standards on Auditing (ISAs), which are used or adopted in more than 130 jurisdictions worldwide.[5] Within this body of standards, ISA 240, "The Auditor's Responsibilities Relating to Fraud in an Audit of Financial Statements," is the key reference point for understanding what an external audit does and does not do with respect to fraud.

ISA 240 requires auditors to:

  • maintain an attitude of professional skepticism throughout the audit, recognizing the possibility that a material misstatement due to fraud could exist notwithstanding past experience with the entity's honesty and integrity;
  • identify and assess the risk of material misstatement due to fraud, using the classic fraud-risk-factor framework (incentive/pressure, opportunity, and rationalization — commonly known as the fraud triangle); and
  • design and perform audit procedures responsive to identified fraud risks, including specific attention to the risk of management override of controls — recognized as a fraud risk present in every entity regardless of the auditor's other risk assessments, because management is uniquely positioned to override controls that otherwise operate effectively.

ISA 240's significance for forensic accounting lies in the boundary it draws: a financial statement audit conducted under the ISAs is designed to obtain reasonable — not absolute — assurance that financial statements are free of material misstatement, and it is not designed primarily to detect fraud. A dedicated forensic accounting investigation, by contrast, is typically commissioned specifically because fraud is suspected or has been discovered, and applies more targeted, fraud-specific procedures than a general-purpose audit. Understanding this distinction is essential when a forensic accountant is asked to explain, in a report or in testimony, why a prior audit did not detect a fraud that a subsequent investigation later uncovered.

6 AICPA Risk Assessment Standards (2006)

In 2007, John L. Colbert's article "What the 10 New Auditing Standards Mean for Forensic Accountants" (The Forensic Examiner, Winter 2007) explained a set of ten new Statements on Auditing Standards (SASs) issued by the AICPA's Auditing Standards Board, applicable to audits for periods beginning on or after December 15, 2006.[6] Eight of these ten SASs are collectively known as the Risk Assessment Standards. Their core requirement is that auditors develop a deeper, more structured understanding of the client entity and its internal control environment — including its business, industry, and regulatory environment — before assessing the risk of material misstatement, whether arising from error or fraud.

For forensic accountants, the practical significance of the Risk Assessment Standards is that they changed the nature and depth of the documentation trail an auditor is required to produce. An auditor operating under these standards must document a more detailed risk assessment and a more explicit linkage between identified risks and the audit procedures performed in response. When fraud is later suspected and a forensic accountant is engaged to investigate, this expanded documentation trail — or its absence — becomes part of what the forensic accountant examines in reconstructing how a fraud may have gone undetected, and can itself become relevant evidence regarding whether prior audit work met applicable professional standards.

7 How the Standards Interact

These several ethics and standards regimes are not redundant; they apply to different roles and different moments in a financial-crime matter. The AICPA Code of Professional Conduct sets the baseline ethical obligations for any CPA, including one working in a forensic capacity. SSCS No. 1 and SSVS No. 1 govern the specific consulting and valuation engagements through which most forensic accounting work is actually delivered. The ACFE Code of Professional Ethics binds Certified Fraud Examiners specifically, and its Canon V prohibition on opining regarding guilt or innocence is the clearest single statement, across all of these codes, of the line between forensic fact-finding and legal judgment. ISA 240 explains what a statutory auditor is and is not obligated to do about fraud risk during a routine audit, providing the baseline against which a subsequent forensic investigation's added value is often measured. And the AICPA's Risk Assessment Standards describe the documentation and risk-assessment discipline auditors have been required to apply since 2006, shaping the evidentiary record a forensic accountant later reviews.

A forensic accountant's report and testimony sit downstream of all five frameworks at once: due care and evidentiary sufficiency (AICPA Code, SSCS No. 1), documented valuation methodology where damages or value conclusions are offered (SSVS No. 1), the confidentiality/disclosure balance and the no-guilt-or-innocence rule (ACFE Code), an understanding of what prior audit work could reasonably have been expected to catch (ISA 240), and an understanding of the risk-assessment documentation trail auditors have produced since 2006 (the AICPA Risk Assessment Standards). Practitioners who depart from these standards — for example, by rendering an opinion on a party's guilt, or by failing to document the basis for a valuation conclusion — expose their work product to exactly the kind of methodological challenge addressed in the Daubert standard and discussed further in Evidence and Testimony.

  3. AICPA Consulting Services Executive Committee. "Statement on Standards for Valuation Services No. 1." 2007. Codified as VS Section 100. https://www.aicpa-cima.com/resources/download/statement-on-standards-for-valuation-services-vs-section-100

Federal Financial Crime Enforcement Agencies

The U.S. federal financial crime enforcement agencies are the primary government bodies responsible for investigating and prosecuting corporate fraud, tax crimes, money laundering, and securities violations in the United States. Three agencies form the operational core of this enforcement architecture: the Federal Bureau of Investigation's (FBI) White-Collar Crime program, the Internal Revenue Service's Criminal Investigation division (IRS-CI), and the Securities and Exchange Commission's (SEC) Division of Enforcement. Each operates under a distinct statutory mandate — criminal investigation, tax-code enforcement, and civil securities regulation, respectively — but the three routinely coordinate through joint task forces, given that most large-scale financial fraud implicates criminal fraud statutes, tax law, and securities law simultaneously.[1][2][3]

Federal Financial Crime Agencies
FBI White-Collar CrimeCriminal investigation; corporate, securities, health care fraud
IRS-CISole criminal authority over Internal Revenue Code violations
SEC Division of EnforcementCivil securities-law enforcement; ~25% of SEC staff
IRS-CI staff~3,000 employees; ~2,100 special agents
IRS-CI conviction rate>90%
IRS-CI FY2025 identified crimes~$10.59 billion
SEC accounting fraud unitFinancial Reporting and Accounting Unit, announced Aug. 2026

See also: AML Statutory Framework, Financial Statement Fraud, Major Fraud Case Studies

1 Overview

Federal financial crime enforcement in the United States is distributed across agencies with different legal tools and burdens of proof rather than concentrated in a single body. The FBI investigates federal crimes and refers cases for criminal prosecution by the Department of Justice; IRS-CI is the only federal agency empowered to investigate criminal violations of the Internal Revenue Code; and the SEC pursues civil enforcement actions (and refers criminal matters to the Department of Justice) against violations of federal securities law.[1][2][3] This division of labor means a single fraud scheme — for example, a public company that falsifies its books, evades tax on the resulting illicit gains, and launders the proceeds — can trigger simultaneous FBI, IRS-CI, and SEC involvement, each pursuing a different legal theory against the same underlying conduct. Historically documented cases such as Enron and WorldCom illustrate this multi-agency pattern, where the SEC filed civil fraud actions while criminal charges proceeded separately through the Department of Justice.

2 FBI White-Collar Crime Program

The FBI's White-Collar Crime program sits within its Criminal Investigative Division and covers a broad jurisdictional portfolio: corporate fraud, securities and commodities fraud, health care fraud, mortgage and financial institution fraud, money laundering, public corruption, and intellectual property theft. The Bureau is the lead federal agency for investigating corporate fraud and health care fraud specifically.[1]

2.1 The Placement-Layering-Integration Model

In its public-facing guidance, the FBI frames money laundering — a crime frequently intertwined with the frauds it investigates — through the classic three-stage model used across the anti-money-laundering (AML) field:

  • Placement — introducing illicit cash or proceeds into the legitimate financial system (for example, depositing criminally derived funds into a bank account).
  • Layering — moving funds through a series of transactions (wire transfers, shell companies, cross-border transfers) designed to obscure their illicit origin and break the audit trail.
  • Integration — reintroducing the now-laundered funds into the economy as apparently legitimate assets, such as real estate, businesses, or investments.

This model underlies both FBI investigative technique and the broader statutory framework discussed in AML Statutory Framework, including the Bank Secrecy Act and the Money Laundering Control Act of 1986 (18 U.S.C. §§ 1956–1957).[1]

2.2 Task Force Partnerships and IC3 Referrals

The FBI's White-Collar Crime program partners with the SEC and IRS through joint task forces on complex fraud matters. For consumer-facing fraud complaints — romance scams, business email compromise, cryptocurrency fraud, and similar online-enabled crime — the Bureau operates the Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) as an intake and referral mechanism, routing complaints toward appropriate investigative units. IC3 referrals function as an early-warning and case-aggregation tool, allowing the Bureau to identify patterns across large numbers of individually small-dollar complaints that might otherwise never surface as a prosecutable case.[1]

3 IRS Criminal Investigation (IRS-CI)

IRS Criminal Investigation is the law-enforcement arm of the Internal Revenue Service and holds a jurisdictional distinction shared by no other federal agency: it is the sole federal authority empowered to investigate criminal violations of the Internal Revenue Code. Its mandate also extends to closely related financial crimes, including money laundering, Bank Secrecy Act (BSA) violations, and terrorist financing.[2]

3.1 History and the Capone Precedent

IRS-CI's investigative approach is most famously associated with the prosecution of organized-crime figure Al Capone, who was convicted on federal tax charges after evading direct prosecution for the underlying violent and racketeering crimes attributed to his organization. This historical episode established a durable enforcement template still cited today: tax-code violations can serve as a prosecutable proxy when the substantive criminal conduct (narcotics trafficking, organized crime, fraud) is otherwise difficult to prove beyond a reasonable doubt.[2]

3.2 Scale and Structure

IRS-CI employs approximately 3,000 employees, of whom roughly 2,100 are special agents, distributed across 20 domestic field offices plus roughly a dozen international attaché posts that support cross-border financial-crime investigations. The division reports a federal conviction rate exceeding 90 percent — a figure IRS-CI attributes to the rigor of its investigative and case-referral process, since cases are typically well-documented through financial records before being referred for prosecution.[2]

3.3 FY2025 Enforcement Results

In fiscal year 2025, IRS-CI identified financial crimes totaling approximately $10.59 billion, a 15.7 percent increase over the figure identified in FY2024. This scale of identified financial crime reflects IRS-CI's expanding role not only in traditional tax-evasion cases but in complex financial-crime investigations that intersect with money laundering, cryptocurrency-based fraud, and organized fraud schemes — the same terrain covered by Financial Statement Fraud and the broader AML statutory framework.[2]

4 SEC Division of Enforcement

The Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Enforcement investigates violations of federal securities laws and litigates civil enforcement actions against individuals and entities. The Division makes up roughly one-quarter of total SEC staff, reflecting enforcement's centrality to the agency's overall mission of protecting investors and maintaining fair, orderly securities markets.[3]

4.1 The Financial Reporting and Accounting Unit

In August 2026, the SEC announced the creation of a new Financial Reporting and Accounting Unit within the Division of Enforcement — the first specialized unit dedicated solely to accounting and financial-reporting fraud and related auditor misconduct. The unit is staffed by both attorneys and accountants, a structural choice that reflects the reality that detecting and proving financial-statement fraud requires forensic-accounting expertise (the kind of transaction-level and journal-entry analysis discussed in Financial Statement Fraud) alongside legal enforcement capability. The unit's creation signals renewed institutional emphasis on accounting-fraud enforcement as a distinct enforcement priority, separate from the SEC's broader securities-fraud and market-manipulation caseload.[3]

4.2 Civil Enforcement Tools

Unlike the FBI and IRS-CI, which pursue criminal investigations that can result in imprisonment, the SEC's enforcement authority is fundamentally civil: it can bring administrative proceedings and federal civil actions seeking remedies such as disgorgement of ill-gotten gains, civil monetary penalties, and injunctions barring individuals from serving as officers or directors of public companies. Where SEC investigators uncover conduct that also appears criminal, the Commission refers those findings to the Department of Justice for potential criminal prosecution — a pattern documented in major cases discussed in Major Fraud Case Studies, where SEC civil actions against corporate officers ran in parallel with separate criminal prosecutions of the same individuals.[3]

5 Interagency Coordination

Because financial fraud schemes routinely violate criminal fraud statutes, the tax code, and securities law simultaneously, the FBI, IRS-CI, and SEC frequently coordinate on the same underlying conduct rather than operating in isolation. Joint task forces allow the agencies to share financial-transaction evidence, deconflict overlapping investigations, and sequence civil and criminal actions so that one does not compromise the other (for example, delaying a civil SEC filing until a parallel criminal investigation reaches an appropriate stage). This coordination model mirrors the placement-layering-integration framework's implicit assumption that illicit financial conduct crosses institutional and jurisdictional lines, which is why no single agency's authority is sufficient on its own to fully address large-scale financial crime.[1][2][3]

6 Relevance to Fraud Examination and OSINT

For open-source investigators and forensic accountants, understanding this three-agency structure clarifies where different categories of evidence and allegation are likely to be actionable. A suspected tax-evasion scheme uncovered through OSINT research on shell companies or offshore transfers falls within IRS-CI's exclusive jurisdiction; suspected market manipulation, insider trading, or falsified public-company financial statements fall within the SEC's civil enforcement remit and, increasingly, the new Financial Reporting and Accounting Unit's specific focus; and broader fraud schemes involving health care billing, mortgage fraud, or public corruption are more likely to fall under the FBI's White-Collar Crime program. Recognizing which agency's mandate a given fact pattern implicates — and that a single complex scheme may implicate all three — is a foundational step in directing a complaint, tip, or referral to the body with actual jurisdiction to act on it.

References

  1. U.S. Federal Bureau of Investigation. "White-Collar Crime." https://www.fbi.gov/investigate/white-collar-crime
  2. U.S. Internal Revenue Service. "About Criminal Investigation." https://www.irs.gov/compliance/criminal-investigation/about-criminal-investigation
  3. U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. "About the Division of Enforcement." https://www.sec.gov/about/divisions-offices/division-enforcement/about-division-enforcement
