Anti-Bribery Law: The FCPA and UK Bribery Act

Anti-Bribery Statutes
U.S. statuteForeign Corrupt Practices Act, 1977
U.S. accounting hook15 U.S.C. §78m(b) — books, records & internal controls
UK statuteBribery Act 2010
UK liability modelSection 7 — strict corporate liability
Sole UK defense"Adequate procedures"
Notable penaltiesSiemens $800M (2008); Alstom $772M (2014)
RelatedAML Statutory Framework, Asset Tracing, Anti-Corruption OSINT

Anti-bribery law is the body of statute that criminalizes the payment or receipt of bribes to secure business advantage, with two regimes dominating international enforcement: the United States' Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA, 1977) and the United Kingdom's Bribery Act 2010. The two statutes take different routes to the same end. The FCPA pairs a narrow anti-bribery prohibition with a broad, independently enforceable requirement that public companies keep accurate books and records and maintain adequate internal accounting controls — a provision that has become the primary vehicle for prosecuting bribery concealed through falsified accounts. The Bribery Act imposes strict corporate criminal liability on any commercial organization that fails to prevent bribery by an associated person, leaving "adequate procedures" as the only statutory defense. Both regimes, in different ways, force a forensic accountant or OSINT investigator to trace payments through third-party intermediaries and shell-company structures, because that is precisely where the bribe is hidden.[1]

1 The FCPA: two provisions, one statute

The Foreign Corrupt Practices Act was enacted in 1977 in the aftermath of Watergate-era investigations that uncovered widespread corporate slush funds used to bribe foreign officials, and it is codified at 15 U.S.C. §§78dd-1 et seq.[2] The statute is best understood as two distinct legal instruments bolted together. The first is the anti-bribery provision, which prohibits U.S. persons, issuers, and certain foreign actors from corruptly offering, promising, or paying anything of value to a foreign official to obtain or retain business. The second, and for forensic-accounting purposes the more consequential, is the accounting provision at 15 U.S.C. §78m(b), added to the statute alongside the anti-bribery language and enforced primarily by the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission rather than the Department of Justice.

This dual structure matters because the two provisions have different reach and different evidentiary demands. The anti-bribery provision requires proof of a corrupt intent to influence a foreign official — a state of mind that can be difficult to establish directly and is usually inferred from circumstantial evidence: the size and timing of a payment relative to a contract award, the involvement of an unexplained intermediary, or communications that thinly veil the purpose of a transaction. The accounting provision requires no such proof of corrupt intent at all. It asks a narrower, more mechanical question: did the company's books accurately reflect what actually happened, and did its internal controls provide reasonable assurance that transactions were recorded properly? Because that question can be answered from the ledger itself, the accounting provision has become the government's preferred charge in cases where bribery is suspected but difficult to prove beyond doubt, or where a settlement is negotiated without an admission of corrupt intent.

2 The accounting provisions in detail

Section 78m(b) imposes two related obligations on any company that registers securities with the SEC or is required to file reports under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 (an "issuer"), regardless of whether the underlying conduct crosses a border at all:

ObligationWhat it requiresWhy it matters for bribery concealment
Books-and-records requirementMake and keep books, records, and accounts that, in reasonable detail, accurately and fairly reflect the transactions and dispositions of the issuer's assetsA bribe is almost never recorded as a bribe; it is disguised as a "consulting fee," "commission," "marketing expense," or "facilitation payment" — the false label itself is the violation
Internal-controls requirementDevise and maintain a system of internal accounting controls sufficient to provide reasonable assurance that transactions are executed and recorded as authorized, and that access to assets is permitted only in accordance with management's authorizationWeak or deliberately unenforced controls — no due diligence on third-party agents, no cap on discretionary payments, no review of intermediary invoices — are what allow disguised bribes to be recorded and paid in the first place

A critical feature of the accounting provisions is that they can be violated — and have repeatedly been charged — without any accompanying anti-bribery charge. A company can settle accounting-provision liability by admitting its books mischaracterized payments and its controls failed to prevent or detect the mischaracterization, without the government having to prove, or the company having to admit, that a specific payment was a corrupt bribe to a specific official. This is a significant strategic reality in FCPA enforcement: the accounting provisions give the SEC a lower evidentiary bar and give companies a path to resolution that avoids the reputational and collateral consequences of an anti-bribery admission. For a forensic accountant, it also reframes the investigative task — the question is not only "was a bribe paid?" but "does the recorded description of this transaction match what actually happened, and did the control environment allow that mismatch to occur?" That framing connects directly to the internal-controls literature discussed under the fraud triangle and to the broader discipline of forensic accounting, where testing the reliability of recorded transactions against underlying reality is the core method regardless of the offense being investigated.

3 Siemens and Alstom: the accounting hook in practice

Two of the largest FCPA resolutions in history illustrate how the accounting provisions operate at scale, and both involved bribery schemes so extensive that off-book concealment became an institutionalized business practice rather than an isolated lapse.

Siemens AG settled FCPA and related charges in December 2008 for approximately $800 million in combined U.S. penalties — at the time the largest FCPA settlement ever — on top of a further roughly $800 million paid to German authorities, for a bribery scheme that U.S. and German investigators found had operated across dozens of countries over a period of years, funded by billions of dollars routed through slush funds, off-book accounts, and consulting arrangements with no genuine business purpose. The scale of the scheme meant that mischaracterized payments and inadequate controls were not incidental to the case; they were the mechanism through which the bribery was funded and concealed, which is why the accounting provisions of the FCPA sat at the center of the resolution alongside the anti-bribery charges.

Alstom SA, the French power-and-transport conglomerate, pleaded guilty and settled FCPA charges in December 2014 for $772 million — the largest criminal fine of its kind by a foreign company at the time — over a bribery scheme spanning multiple countries in which the company used consultants and intermediaries to funnel bribes to government officials to win power, grid, and transportation contracts. As with Siemens, the payments to intermediaries were recorded on Alstom's books as legitimate consulting or agency fees, and the case turned in significant part on establishing that those descriptions were false and that the company's internal controls did not catch, and in some tellings actively facilitated, the mischaracterization.

Both cases share a structural lesson: penalties of this magnitude are not simply the price of bribery itself but reflect years of accumulated accounting-provision exposure — every mischaracterized invoice and every control failure is, in principle, its own instance of non-compliance, and prosecutors and regulators have consistently used that cumulative exposure as leverage in negotiating record-setting settlements.

4 The UK Bribery Act 2010

The Bribery Act 2010 came into force in the United Kingdom in July 2011 and is frequently described as a stricter and more far-reaching anti-corruption statute than the FCPA. It creates four principal offenses: bribing another person (Section 1), being bribed (Section 2), bribing a foreign public official (Section 6), and — the provision of greatest relevance to corporate compliance and forensic investigation — the corporate offense of failure of a commercial organization to prevent bribery (Section 7).

Two features distinguish the Bribery Act from the FCPA. First, the Act contains no exception for "facilitation payments" — small payments to expedite routine government action, which occupy a narrow but real carve-out under the FCPA. Under the Bribery Act, a facilitation payment is simply a bribe. Second, the Act reaches private-sector, commercial bribery as well as bribery of public officials, whereas the FCPA's anti-bribery provisions are limited to payments to foreign government officials. Both features tend to widen the population of transactions a compliance or investigative review must examine.

5 Section 7 and the adequate-procedures defense

Section 7 is the Bribery Act's most consequential innovation for corporate liability. It makes a "relevant commercial organization" — broadly, any UK company or partnership, and any foreign company that carries on business or part of a business in the UK — criminally liable if a person "associated with" the organization (an employee, agent, subsidiary, or other party performing services for or on the organization's behalf) bribes another person intending to obtain or retain business or a business advantage for the organization.

The offense is strict liability in the sense that the organization need not have known about, authorized, or intended the bribery; the prosecution does not have to prove corporate fault or knowledge at all, only that an associated person committed bribery for the organization's benefit. This is a materially lower bar than the FCPA's anti-bribery provisions, which generally require proof that the company itself, through its officers or with corporate knowledge, engaged in or authorized the corrupt conduct. Under Section 7, a single rogue agent's bribe, paid without any executive's knowledge, can expose the entire organization to criminal liability.

Against that strict standard, Parliament provided exactly one statutory defense: the organization can avoid conviction if it proves it had "adequate procedures" in place designed to prevent persons associated with it from engaging in bribery. The UK Ministry of Justice's statutory guidance on adequate procedures sets out six principles commonly cited in compliance practice: proportionate procedures, top-level commitment, risk assessment, due diligence on associated persons, communication and training, and monitoring and review. Because the defense places the burden on the organization to prove its procedures were adequate, Section 7 functions less like a traditional criminal offense and more like a compliance-audit standard backed by criminal sanction — an organization is, in effect, required to be able to demonstrate a documented, risk-based anti-bribery compliance program at the time an offense is alleged, not construct one afterward.

6 Why these statutes require tracing intermediaries and shells

Both statutes converge on the same investigative reality: bribery is rarely paid directly from a company to an official. It is routed through third-party intermediaries — sales agents, consultants, distributors, joint-venture partners — and frequently through shell-company structures that exist to receive a payment, obscure its ultimate destination, and generate a paper trail that superficially resembles a legitimate commercial transaction. This is not incidental to bribery schemes; it is the mechanism that makes the FCPA's accounting-provision violation and the Bribery Act's Section 7 exposure possible in the first place, because a direct, undisguised payment from a company to a foreign official would be trivially detectable and would leave no ambiguity for a mischaracterized "consulting fee" to exploit.

For a forensic accountant or investigator, this means the anti-bribery statutes cannot be enforced, and cannot be investigated, by reading the anti-bribery clause in isolation. The practical work is asset tracing applied to the corruption context: identifying intermediaries who received payments disproportionate to any plausible legitimate service, testing whether an entity has the substance of a real business (employees, premises, an arm's-length pricing history) or is a shell with none of these, and following the funds an intermediary received to see whether they were passed on, in whole or in part, to an official or the official's associates. This is precisely the beneficial-ownership and shell-company detection work described in Asset Tracing, and it is reinforced from the open-source side by the techniques described in Anti-Corruption OSINT — watchlist and adverse-media screening of intermediaries, and the observation that officials and their relatives frequently display unexplained wealth publicly, which can corroborate a paper trail that a shell structure was built to obscure. Because both the FCPA's accounting provisions and the Bribery Act's Section 7 liability turn on documentary and control evidence rather than solely on proof of a corrupt handshake, the investigator's product in an anti-bribery matter looks very much like a standard forensic accounting engagement: a link-by-link, document-supported narrative connecting a recorded payment to its true economic substance.

7 FCPA and Bribery Act compared

FeatureFCPA (US, 1977)Bribery Act (UK, 2010)
Core anti-bribery targetForeign government officialsAny person; separate offense for foreign public officials
Private-sector (commercial) briberyNot covered by anti-bribery provisionsCovered (Sections 1 & 2)
Facilitation paymentsNarrow statutory exceptionNo exception — treated as bribery
Corporate liability standardGenerally requires corporate knowledge/authorization for anti-bribery liabilityStrict liability for failure to prevent bribery by an associated person (Section 7)
Available defenseNo equivalent standalone defense to anti-bribery liability"Adequate procedures" — the sole statutory defense to Section 7
Distinct accounting exposureYes — 15 U.S.C. §78m(b), enforced by the SEC, no proof of corrupt intent requiredNo direct equivalent; UK companies with US issuer status may face both regimes
Primary enforcersDOJ (anti-bribery, criminal) and SEC (accounting provisions, civil/administrative)Serious Fraud Office and Crown Prosecution Service

The practical consequence of this comparison is that a multinational company can face parallel exposure under both regimes for the same underlying conduct — a UK-incorporated subsidiary's failure to prevent an agent's bribery can trigger Section 7 liability for the group, while the same mischaracterized payment on a US-listed parent's consolidated books can independently trigger FCPA accounting-provision liability, without either regulator needing to prove the other's elements.

8 See also

See also: AML Statutory Framework, Asset Tracing, Anti-Corruption OSINT, Forensic Accounting, Fraud Triangle.

The AML Statutory Framework: BSA, MLCA, FinCEN, and FATF

AML Statutory Framework
Foundational statuteBank Secrecy Act (1970)
Criminalizing statuteMoney Laundering Control Act (1986), 18 U.S.C. §§1956–1957
U.S. regulatorFinCEN (Treasury bureau)
International standard-setterFATF (founded 1989)
SAR filing deadline30 days (60 if no suspect identified)
SAR threshold$5,000 general / $2,000 MSBs
RelatedAML Law, Money Laundering, Asset Tracing

See also: AML Law (the broader legal survey, including AMLA 2020 and the Corporate Transparency Act), Money Laundering (the underlying placement–layering–integration mechanics), and Asset Tracing (following funds once a report or investigation exists).

The AML statutory framework is the specific set of laws and institutions that (1) define money laundering as a distinct federal crime, (2) require regulated businesses to report cash and suspicious transactions to the government, and (3) set the international benchmark against which national anti-money-laundering (AML) regimes are judged. This article stays close to the founding statutory text and the regulatory mechanics beneath it: what the Bank Secrecy Act requires of a financial institution, what the Money Laundering Control Act criminalizes, when and how a Suspicious Activity Report (SAR) must be filed, and how the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) built and enforces its global standard. It closes with adjacent statutes — RICO, the False Claims Act, and the Computer Fraud and Abuse Act — that frequently appear alongside AML charges.[1]

1 Division of coverage with AML Law

Because this wiki already carries a general AML Law article, the two pages are deliberately scoped to avoid repeating each other. AML Law covers the CTR/SAR distinction at survey level, FATF's 40 Recommendations as a topic heading, the 2020 Anti-Money Laundering Act (AMLA) and its whistleblower bounty, and the 2026 Corporate Transparency Act beneficial-ownership rollback — the parts of the framework that have changed most recently. This article goes underneath those headlines to the founding statutes: the specific reporting and retention duties the Bank Secrecy Act imposes, the exact criminal elements of 18 U.S.C. §§1956 and 1957, the procedural detail of SAR filing (deadlines, thresholds, and the statutory confidentiality shield), and FATF's institutional history and enforcement mechanics rather than just its Recommendations. It also treats RICO, the False Claims Act, and the CFAA in more depth, including the Supreme Court's 2021 narrowing of CFAA liability in Van Buren v. United States. Readers wanting AMLA 2020, the whistleblower program, or the current status of beneficial-ownership reporting should consult AML Law directly; this article does not restate those provisions.

2 The Bank Secrecy Act of 1970

The Currency and Foreign Transactions Reporting Act of 1970, universally known as the Bank Secrecy Act (BSA), is the foundational U.S. anti-money-laundering statute, codified at 31 U.S.C. §§5311 et seq. Congress designed it to generate a paper trail with a "high degree of usefulness" in criminal, tax, and regulatory investigations by imposing recordkeeping and reporting duties directly on financial institutions rather than waiting to prosecute laundering after the fact.[2]

The BSA's core obligations are:

  • Currency Transaction Reports (CTRs), filed on FinCEN Form 112, for cash transactions exceeding $10,000 in a single business day;
  • Suspicious Activity Reports (SARs), covering transactions that suggest money laundering or other criminal conduct regardless of dollar amount (detailed in Section 4 below);
  • recordkeeping for cash purchases of negotiable instruments and other transactions the statute identifies as evidentially significant; and
  • a minimum five-year retention period for the records the BSA requires institutions to keep — the baseline duration an investigator can expect a bank, money services business, or other covered institution to still hold historical transaction records.[2]

The BSA has been amended substantially since 1970, most significantly by Title III of the USA PATRIOT Act (2001), which layered on customer due diligence and beneficial-ownership requirements, and by the 2020 AMLA, discussed in AML Law. But the 1970 Act remains the statutory root: every reporting duty a modern bank, broker-dealer, or money services business (MSB) discharges through FinCEN ultimately traces back to the BSA's original recordkeeping-and-reporting mandate.[2]

3 The Money Laundering Control Act of 1986

The BSA required reporting, but for its first sixteen years U.S. law did not treat laundering money as a freestanding federal crime in itself — prosecutors had to reach it indirectly, through the underlying predicate offense or through structuring and reporting violations. The Money Laundering Control Act of 1986 (MLCA), enacted as part of that year's Anti-Drug Abuse Act, closed that gap. It was the first U.S. federal statute to criminalize money laundering as its own offense, codified at 18 U.S.C. §§1956–1957.[3]

ProvisionPopular nameCore elementsMinimum threshold
18 U.S.C. §1956Laundering of Monetary InstrumentsKnowingly conducting a financial transaction involving proceeds of "specified unlawful activity," with intent to (a) promote the crime, (b) conceal the proceeds' nature, source, or ownership, (c) avoid a reporting requirement, or (d) evade taxesNone — no minimum dollar amount and no requirement that a financial institution be involved
18 U.S.C. §1957Engaging in Monetary Transactions in Property Derived from Unlawful ActivityKnowingly engaging in a monetary transaction in criminally derived property, without the concealment-intent element §1956 requiresTransaction must exceed $10,000

The contrast between the two sections is one of the more consequential technical distinctions in AML law. Section 1956 is the broader and more serious provision: it requires proof of one of the four specific intents listed above, but in exchange it applies to any transaction amount and does not require the transaction to pass through a bank or other financial institution at all — a cash handoff in a parking lot can violate §1956 if the intent element is met. Section 1957 is narrower and easier to prove in one respect (no concealment intent is required, only knowledge that the property is criminally derived) but is bounded by its $10,000 threshold. Maximum penalties under the MLCA reach twenty years' imprisonment. Because §1956 carries no floor, it is the provision most often paired with lower-dollar structuring and layering conduct that would otherwise fall outside the BSA's $10,000 CTR trigger — the deliberate splitting of transactions described as a classic red flag in the placement stage of laundering, as covered in Money Laundering.[3]

4 FinCEN and the SAR filing regime

The Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN), a bureau of the U.S. Department of the Treasury, administers the Bank Secrecy Act and defines the operative rules that turn the BSA's general SAR mandate into a concrete filing regime. Three sets of details matter to an investigator relying on SAR data: who must file, on what deadline, and what confidentiality protects the report once filed.[4]

Trigger and thresholds. A covered institution must file a SAR when it knows, suspects, or has reason to suspect that a transaction conducted by, at, or through the institution is suspicious, and the transaction involves at least $5,000 — or, for money services businesses (MSBs), at least $2,000. Unlike the CTR, which triggers on a flat currency threshold, the SAR trigger turns on the institution's subjective and objective suspicion, so a transaction well under $10,000 can still generate a mandatory filing.[4]

Filing deadlines. FinCEN's SAR deadlines are staged around detection and ongoing activity:

DeadlineApplies when
30 calendar daysFrom initial detection of facts constituting the basis for filing (the default deadline)
60 calendar daysExtension permitted when no suspect has been identified as of the 30-day mark
120 daysGeneral guideline for filing a continuing-activity SAR relative to the prior related filing, where suspicious activity persists

Confidentiality. SAR confidentiality is not a matter of institutional policy; it is a statutory mandate under 31 U.S.C. §5318(g)(2), which prohibits a financial institution (and its directors, officers, employees, and agents) from disclosing that a SAR has been filed, or disclosing any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR, to the subject of the report or to any other unauthorized party. This confidentiality shield is what allows SARs to function as an intelligence product for law enforcement and FinCEN rather than a notice to the person under suspicion, and it is why SAR filings themselves are not directly obtainable through ordinary discovery or public-records requests — a practical limit worth understanding before attempting to build an asset-tracing workflow that assumes SAR access.[4]

FinCEN also administers CTR filing (Form 112, discussed in Section 2) and beneficial-ownership reporting under the Corporate Transparency Act, whose 2026 status is covered separately in AML Law.

5 FATF: founding, Recommendations, and lists

The Financial Action Task Force (FATF) is the intergovernmental policy-making body that sets the global AML and counter-terrorist-financing (CFT) standard against which national regimes — including the U.S. framework described above — are measured. FATF was established in 1989 at the G7 Paris Summit, initially to address the emerging problem of money laundering tied to drug trafficking. Its mandate expanded in 2001, after the September 11 attacks, to cover terrorist financing, and later still to cover financing of weapons proliferation.[5]

FATF's principal instrument is "The FATF Recommendations," commonly called the 40 Recommendations. First issued in 1990, they were revised in 1996, revised again in 2003, and comprehensively rewritten in 2012, with ongoing amendments since (the companion AML Law article notes a February 2025 amendment to Recommendation 1). The Recommendations are not self-executing treaty law; they are a standard that member and cooperating jurisdictions are expected to transpose into their own domestic statutes — which is exactly the relationship between FATF's standard and the U.S. BSA/MLCA regime described in Sections 2–3 above.[5]

A central methodological pillar of the modern Recommendations is the risk-based approach (RBA): rather than mandating identical, one-size-fits-all controls for every institution and transaction type, the RBA requires countries and individual institutions to first identify, assess, and understand their own money-laundering and terrorist-financing risks, and then apply AML/CFT measures proportionate to those risks — concentrating scrutiny and resources where the danger is highest rather than spreading them evenly. This is the same logic that underlies why a bank's due-diligence intensity varies by customer type, geography, and product, rather than being uniform across every account.[5]

FATF enforces its standard not through direct sanctions but through peer-review mutual evaluations, in which member and FATF-Style Regional Body (FSRB) jurisdictions assess one another's compliance and effectiveness. Countries found to have strategic AML/CFT deficiencies are publicly named on one of two lists:

  • the "black list" (formally, "High-Risk Jurisdictions subject to a Call for Action") — countries with the most serious, unaddressed deficiencies, prompting FATF to call on all members to apply enhanced due diligence or, in the most serious cases, countermeasures; and
  • the "grey list" (formally, "Jurisdictions under Increased Monitoring") — countries that have committed to an action plan to resolve identified deficiencies and are being actively monitored while they do so.

Listing carries real practical consequences: banks and other regulated institutions worldwide typically apply enhanced due diligence, and sometimes de-risk entirely, when dealing with counterparties in listed jurisdictions, which can materially restrict a country's access to the global financial system. FATF's standard now reaches more than 180 countries through its own membership plus its network of FATF-Style Regional Bodies, making it the closest thing the AML field has to a single global baseline — and the reason an investigator tracing funds across borders, as described in Asset Tracing, should always check a jurisdiction's current FATF status before assuming its domestic AML controls resemble those of the FATF founding members.[5]

6 Adjacent statutes: RICO, False Claims Act, CFAA

Money-laundering charges are rarely brought in isolation. Three additional statutes recur often enough alongside BSA/MLCA charges, and in broader financial-crime and digital-evidence investigations generally, that an investigator should recognize them even though each is a subject in its own right rather than a core AML statute.[6]

StatuteYearRelevance to AML investigations
RICO (Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act, 18 U.S.C. §§1961–1968)1970Criminalizes conducting or investing in an enterprise through a "pattern of racketeering activity" (at least two predicate acts within ten years). Money laundering is itself a recognized RICO predicate, so a pattern of laundering transactions can support a RICO count layered on top of, or instead of, standalone §1956/§1957 charges. Civil RICO additionally allows private plaintiffs to recover treble damages.
False Claims Act (31 U.S.C. §§3729–3733)1863 (substantially amended 1986)The government's primary civil tool against fraud on federal programs and contracts. Its qui tam provisions let private relators sue on the government's behalf and share in the recovery — a whistleblower-incentive structure conceptually similar to the AMLA bounty program described in AML Law. False Claims Act proceeds are themselves a common predicate for downstream laundering charges when a fraud recovery is concealed or moved.
Computer Fraud and Abuse Act (CFAA) (18 U.S.C. §1030)1986Criminalizes unauthorized access to a "protected computer," a term defined broadly enough to reach essentially any internet-connected device. Financial-crime investigations increasingly rely on the CFAA where laundering or fraud schemes involve compromised accounts or systems.

The CFAA's reach was narrowed significantly by the Supreme Court in Van Buren v. United States (2021), which addressed the statute's "exceeds authorized access" language — the provision covering someone who has legitimate access to a computer system but uses that access for an improper purpose. The Court rejected the broader reading under which any misuse of otherwise-authorized access (for example, an employee who looks up a database record for a personal, non-work reason) could trigger CFAA liability, holding instead that liability attaches only when a person accesses information or areas of a computer system that are off-limits to them, not merely when they misuse access they are otherwise entitled to have. For an OSINT or forensic investigator, Van Buren is the operative boundary line: it clarifies how far a researcher or examiner with legitimate credentials to a system may go, in terms of what is actually accessible to them, before crossing from a permissible-use question into unlawful "exceeding authorized access" territory — a distinction that matters whenever a fraud investigation touches an employer's own systems or a cooperating institution's records.[6]

Money Laundering

Money Laundering
DefinitionDisguising the illicit origin of criminal proceeds
StagesPlacement → Layering → Integration
Est. scaleFinancial crime part of $4.4T illicit funds (2025)[6]
Standard-setterFATF (est. 1989)
U.S. regulatorFinCEN, under the Bank Secrecy Act (1970)
Key filingsCTR (cash > $10,000), SAR

Money laundering is the process of disguising the origins of money obtained through crime so that it appears to derive from legitimate sources. It is the financial engine that lets predicate offenses — drug trafficking, corruption, fraud, human trafficking, and organized crime — pay, and defeating it is the object of the global anti-money-laundering (AML) regime. Because laundering deliberately breaks the link between funds and their criminal source, it is the central obstacle that asset tracing and the net worth method are built to overcome.[1]

1 Definition

Money laundering converts "dirty" money — proceeds of crime — into apparently clean funds that can be spent, invested, or moved through the legitimate financial system without attracting suspicion. The launderer's goals are to distance the money from its criminal origin, to obscure the audit trail, and ultimately to enjoy or reinvest the proceeds openly. Laundering is itself a distinct offense in most jurisdictions, separate from the underlying (predicate) crime, and the same mechanics recur whether the source is narcotics, embezzlement, sanctions evasion, or tax fraud. The internationally recognized model describes laundering as unfolding in three stages, though in practice the stages overlap and a single scheme may cycle through them repeatedly.[2]

The term "laundering" reflects the objective: to run dirty money through a process that emerges looking clean. Definitions vary in wording across jurisdictions but converge on the same elements — knowingly dealing with the proceeds of crime in a way designed to conceal or disguise their illicit origin, ownership, or control, or to help someone evade the legal consequences of the predicate offense. Some legal frameworks also criminalize the reverse flow (moving clean money to fund crime, particularly terrorism), which is why the field is usually paired as AML/CFT — anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism. Money laundering matters to fraud examiners even when the predicate crime is well understood, because the laundering trail is often the most durable evidence and the best route to recovering assets for victims.

2 The three stages

The placement–layering–integration model, derived from the Financial Action Task Force and endorsed by bodies such as FinCEN and UNODC, is the standard framework for understanding how laundering works.[2]

StageWhat happensTypical examplesVulnerability to detection
PlacementIllicit cash first enters the financial systemDepositing cash below reporting thresholds (structuring), buying casino chips, mixing cash through a cash-intensive front business, purchasing money ordersHighest — cash is closest to the crime; CTR and SAR filings target this stage
LayeringFunds are moved and converted through many transactions to break the audit trailChains of wire transfers, shell-company invoicing, buying and reselling assets, cross-border transfers, chain-hopping between cryptocurrenciesModerate — complexity hides origin but each hop leaves records
IntegrationLaundered funds re-enter the economy as apparently legitimate wealthReal-estate purchases, business investment, luxury goods, loans to oneself from a shell, salary from a front companyLowest — money now looks clean; detection relies on lifestyle and net-worth analysis

At placement, a drug operation might feed cash proceeds through a chain of laundromats or restaurants, blending it with genuine takings. At layering, that money is wired to an offshore shell, used to "buy" consulting services from a related company, and converted into and out of digital assets to fragment the trail. At integration, the now-obscured funds buy an apartment building or are paid back as a salary or a loan, giving the launderer a clean explanation for their wealth. Because integration produces visible assets against invisible income, it is precisely where the net worth method bites.

The three-stage model is a useful teaching device rather than a rigid sequence. Real schemes blur the boundaries: a cash-intensive front business performs placement and the first step of integration simultaneously, and cryptocurrency can compress placement and layering into a single set of transactions. Investigators nonetheless value the model because it maps neatly onto where each stage is most exposed. Placement is the riskiest moment for the launderer — the funds are still cash, physically close to the crime, and this is where mandatory reporting (the Currency Transaction Report and Suspicious Activity Report) is designed to catch them. Layering is where complexity is deliberately manufactured, so it is attacked with link and network analysis and, on blockchains, with address clustering. Integration is the hardest to detect transactionally because the money now looks legitimate, which is why lifestyle and net-worth analysis — measuring wealth against declared income — becomes the tool of choice at that stage.

3 Common techniques

Launderers combine a familiar toolkit, adapting it to the sums involved and the sophistication of the network.

  • Smurfing / structuring — breaking a large cash sum into many deposits each kept under the $10,000 Currency Transaction Report threshold, often across multiple people ("smurfs"), accounts, and branches. Structuring is itself illegal and is a defined trigger for a Suspicious Activity Report.[3]
  • Front and cash-intensive businesses — legitimate-looking businesses with high cash turnover (restaurants, car washes, nail salons, vending) into which illicit cash is co-mingled and reported as revenue.
  • Hawala / fei ch'ien — informal value-transfer systems that move money across borders through trusted brokers who settle among themselves, leaving little or no conventional banking record.
  • Trade-based money laundering — moving value by mis-invoicing trade: over- or under-valuing goods, multiple invoicing, or shipping phantom goods so that value crosses borders disguised as commerce.
  • Shell companies — entities with no genuine operations used to hold assets, issue fake invoices, and obscure beneficial ownership behind layers of corporate structure.
  • Casinos — buying chips with dirty cash, gambling minimally, then cashing out for a check that appears to be gambling winnings.
  • Cryptocurrency — using digital assets, mixers/tumblers, privacy coins, and chain-hopping to layer funds; the countermeasures are covered under cryptocurrency tracing.

These techniques are not mutually exclusive; a mature laundering operation stacks several — for instance structuring cash into a front business, invoicing it out through shells via trade mis-pricing, and integrating the result into real estate. The choice of technique is driven by the volume of funds, the sophistication of the network, and the enforcement environment. Small-scale schemes may rely on structuring and a single front; transnational organizations combine hawala corridors, trade mis-invoicing across jurisdictions, and layered shell structures spanning secrecy-friendly jurisdictions. Trade-based laundering in particular is difficult to detect because it hides inside the enormous legitimate volume of international commerce, and because verifying the "true" value of goods across borders is genuinely hard. Real estate is a favored integration vehicle worldwide precisely because property is high-value, can be bought through corporate vehicles that obscure the buyer, and tends to hold or appreciate in value — allowing the launderer to store wealth while manufacturing an innocent explanation for it.

4 Scale

Money laundering is measured in trillions. The Nasdaq Verafin Global Financial Crime Report estimated more than $3.1 trillion in illicit funds flowed through the global financial system in 2023, and its newest edition puts the figure at $4.4 trillion for 2025 — an increase of $1.3 trillion since 2023, a compound annual growth rate of about 19.2% — with combined bank-fraud and scam losses of $579.4 billion in 2025 and 90% of institutions reporting increased AI-driven attacks.[6] Estimates of laundering are inherently uncertain because the activity is designed to be invisible, but even conservative figures place it among the largest categories of financial crime and well beyond the reach of any single enforcement body. The United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime has long cited a working estimate that 2–5% of global GDP is laundered annually, a range that translates into figures comparable to the Verafin totals. Any such figure is necessarily an approximation built on the proceeds of detected crime scaled up by assumptions about what remains hidden; the practical takeaway is not the precise number but the order of magnitude, which is large enough to distort markets, corrupt institutions, and fund further crime. The reported acceleration in AI-enabled fraud and scams adds a newer dimension, as generative tools lower the cost of the fraud that generates proceeds in the first place.[6]

5 Enforcement and the AML regime

The response is a layered international and national framework detailed under AML Law. The Financial Action Task Force (FATF), established in 1989, is the intergovernmental standard-setter; its 40 Recommendations (issued 1990, revised 2012, and amended through 2025) define the global AML/CFT baseline that member states implement.[4] In the United States, the Bank Secrecy Act (BSA, 1970) is the foundational statute, administered by the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN). Two reporting mechanisms anchor U.S. detection: the Currency Transaction Report (CTR), filed on FinCEN Form 112 for cash transactions exceeding $10,000 in a business day (filed within 15 days and retained five years), and the Suspicious Activity Report (SAR), filed when an institution detects suspicious activity, including structuring.[3] The Anti-Money Laundering Act of 2020 (effective January 1, 2021) delivered the most significant AML reform in decades, including a FinCEN whistleblower bounty of up to 30%.[5] Beneficial-ownership reporting under the Corporate Transparency Act has since been sharply curtailed: a FinCEN final rule of August 2026 permanently removed the reporting requirement for U.S. companies and U.S. persons, leaving only certain foreign-registered entities as reporting companies — a change with direct consequences for asset-tracing investigators who relied on that data.[5]

See also: AML Law, Asset Tracing, Cryptocurrency Tracing, Net Worth Method, Human Trafficking Finance.

  2. Financial Action Task Force / FinCEN / UNODC. Placement–Layering–Integration model. (FATF-derived; endorsed by FinCEN and UNODC)
  3. FinCEN. Bank Secrecy Act reporting — Currency Transaction Report and Suspicious Activity Report. fincen.gov

Asset Tracing

Asset tracing is the investigative process of following the money — reconstructing the movement of funds and property from a known point (a fraud, a bribe, a laundering scheme) to where the value ultimately resides, so that it can be identified, frozen, and recovered. It is the operational counterpart to money laundering: where laundering breaks the trail, asset tracing rebuilds it. The discipline combines document analysis, analytical methods, open-source intelligence, and legal instruments for obtaining records across borders.[1]

1 Following the money

The premise of asset tracing is that money leaves a trail. Every transfer, deposit, purchase, and title change generates a record somewhere, and even a deliberately layered scheme must at some point connect the illicit source to a real beneficiary who wants to use the funds. The investigator works forward from the source of the funds or backward from a suspected asset, building a chain of transactions in which each link is supported by a document. Because laundering is designed to defeat this reconstruction, tracing is frequently combined with the indirect net worth method, which measures the wealth that lands with a subject regardless of the path it took — a useful cross-check when the transactional trail goes cold.[1]

Two legal concepts shape what tracing can achieve. Following the money means tracking the same physical or electronic funds as they move; tracing in the strict legal sense allows an investigator to follow value into a substitute asset — when stolen funds are used to buy a house, the claim can attach to the house. This matters for recovery, because assets are rarely sitting in the account they first landed in by the time an investigation matures. Tracing is used both in criminal proceedings (to support confiscation and forfeiture of the proceeds of crime) and in civil litigation (to recover misappropriated funds for victims and creditors), and the standard of proof and available remedies differ between the two. In either setting the investigator's product is a documented, link-by-link narrative that a court can follow and that survives challenge.

2 Bank and wire record analysis

Bank records are the backbone of most traces. Account statements establish the flow of funds; wire-transfer records (including SWIFT messages and Fedwire data) reveal counterparties, correspondent banks, and cross-border movement. The investigator reconstructs the flow of funds — mapping which account fed which, in what amounts, and on what dates — and looks for the hallmarks of layering: rapid pass-through transfers, round-dollar movements, transfers to and from shell entities, and structuring below the $10,000 reporting threshold that triggers a Currency Transaction Report. Link and network analysis is central here, visualizing accounts and entities as a graph so that pass-through nodes and ultimate destinations stand out. Suspicious Activity Reports filed by institutions can provide investigative leads, and subpoenaed transaction data feeds the same graph.

The analytical challenge is that layering produces a large, noisy graph in which the meaningful path is buried among ordinary business flows. Investigators therefore triage: they prioritize round-dollar and same-day pass-through transfers, transactions that leave an account almost as fast as they arrive, funds routed to jurisdictions with weak transparency, and counterparties with no evident commercial relationship to the account holder. Timing analysis — matching the moment funds leave one account to when they appear in another — helps establish links that the accounts themselves do not label. Where an institution's own monitoring flagged the activity, the resulting Suspicious Activity Report can name counterparties and describe the pattern that first drew attention, giving the investigator a starting thread to pull.

3 Source documents

Statements summarize; source documents prove. Tracing relies on the primary records behind each transaction, because these tie a movement of money to a specific person and instruction. Key source documents include:

DocumentWhat it establishes
Signature cardsWho is authorized on an account — identifying the real controllers behind nominee or shell accounts
Cancelled checksPayer, payee, endorsements, and the bank of deposit — linking a payment to its ultimate recipient
Deposit tickets / slipsThe composition of a deposit (cash vs. checks) and its date, exposing structuring and co-mingling
Wire-transfer recordsOriginator, beneficiary, intermediary banks, and reference details across borders
Account-opening documentsBeneficial-owner declarations, identification, and the customer due-diligence file

Endorsements on cancelled checks and the deposit history behind them frequently break a scheme open, revealing that a "vendor" payment was in fact endorsed over to an insider or deposited into a personal account. These records are the same class of primary evidence that must be preserved under a documented chain of custody to survive challenge in court.

4 Shell-company detection and beneficial ownership

Layering typically runs value through shell companies — entities with no genuine operations that exist to hold assets, issue false invoices, and hide who ultimately benefits. Detecting them combines transactional signals (no employees or premises, round-number invoicing, funds that arrive and leave the same day, addresses shared with other shells or with an insider) and corporate-registry research. The investigative goal is to pierce the structure to the beneficial owner — the natural person who ultimately owns or controls the entity. Beneficial-ownership data has historically been a key tracing resource, but its availability shifted sharply in the United States: after litigation and an interim rule, a FinCEN final rule of August 2026 permanently removed beneficial-ownership reporting for U.S. companies and U.S. persons, leaving only certain foreign-registered entities as reporting companies.[2] Investigators increasingly supplement registry data with open-source intelligence — corporate filings, leaks, litigation records, and name-screening tools — to reconstruct ownership that formal registers no longer capture.[3]

5 Cross-border recovery and MLATs

Laundered funds rarely stay in one jurisdiction, so recovery is inherently international. Once assets are located abroad, investigators pursue freezing (restraint) orders and, ultimately, confiscation or forfeiture and repatriation. Obtaining foreign evidence and enforcement generally proceeds through Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties (MLATs) — bilateral or multilateral agreements under which one state formally requests another to gather evidence, serve process, freeze accounts, or enforce a confiscation order. MLAT requests are powerful but slow and formal, which is why investigators front-load open-source and informal channels — and faster law-enforcement cooperation such as the Egmont Group network of financial intelligence units — to build the picture before a treaty request is made. Timing is critical: assets that are not restrained early can be moved again or dissipated while a formal request works through diplomatic channels, so investigators often pursue urgent freezing orders in parallel with the slower evidence-gathering process. International bodies such as the OSCE have published compendiums of good practice on financial investigations and asset recovery, particularly in the context of proceeds-generating crimes like human trafficking.[1] Where the trail runs through digital assets, tracing continues on-chain under cryptocurrency tracing, with exchange attribution and law-enforcement cooperation substituting for the bank subpoena.

See also: Money Laundering, Cryptocurrency Tracing, Net Worth Method, Analytical Methods, AML Law.

References

  1. OSCE. "Following the Money: Compendium of Resources and Good Practices on Financial Investigations Related to Trafficking in Human Beings." 2019.
  2. FinCEN. Final rule on Beneficial Ownership Information reporting, effective Aug 14, 2026. federalregister.gov
  3. Basel Institute on Governance. "Quick Guide to Open-Source Intelligence" (QG 17), 2020; Basel Open Intelligence. baselgovernance.org

Cryptocurrency Tracing

Cryptocurrency Tracing
FieldBlockchain analytics / forensic accounting
Core methodsAddress clustering, transaction heuristics, exchange attribution
Leading toolsChainalysis, Elliptic, TRM Labs (all active)
Notable exitCipherTrace — key products shut by Mastercard, Mar 2024
ObfuscationPrivacy coins, mixers/tumblers, chain-hopping

Cryptocurrency tracing applies the logic of asset tracing to blockchains: it follows the movement of digital assets across public ledgers to identify counterparties, cluster addresses under common control, and attribute funds to real-world entities such as exchanges. Because most major blockchains (Bitcoin, Ethereum) are pseudonymous rather than anonymous — every transaction is permanently public — they are in some respects easier to trace than cash, provided the investigator can connect on-chain addresses to off-chain identities. The field, known as blockchain analytics, has become a standard component of money-laundering and fraud investigations.[1]

1 How tracing works

Tracing rests on three linked techniques. Address clustering groups the many addresses that a single actor controls into one entity, most commonly via the common-input-ownership heuristic: when several addresses jointly fund one transaction, they are presumed to share an owner. Heuristics then refine the picture — change-address detection identifies which output returns to the sender, and behavioral patterns distinguish exchanges, mixers, and merchant wallets. Exchange attribution is the step that connects the pseudonymous graph to identity: when traced funds reach a regulated exchange that performs Know-Your-Customer (KYC) checks, investigators can, with legal process, obtain the account holder's identity. In effect, the launderer's need to eventually cash out through a regulated on-ramp or off-ramp is the choke point that makes blockchain tracing work, mirroring how conventional asset tracing relies on the point where value re-enters the banking system. Investigators combine on-chain analysis with open-source intelligence — forum posts, leaked databases, and address labels — to strengthen attribution.

A crucial distinction is between pseudonymity and anonymity. On a transparent blockchain, an address is a pseudonym: it does not name its owner, but every transaction it makes is public and permanent, so a single confirmed link between an address and an identity can retroactively expose the entire history of funds that passed through it. This "unmasking" property is what makes public blockchains, in some respects, more traceable than cash — cash leaves no ledger at all. The analytics firms maintain large, continuously updated databases of address labels (exchanges, mixers, darknet markets, sanctioned entities, ransomware wallets) built from their own research, subpoena returns, undercover transactions, and public data. The quality of a trace depends heavily on the quality of this labeling, which is why attribution is both the most valuable and the most contestable part of the process — a point underscored by the reliability concerns that surrounded the CipherTrace shutdown discussed below.

2 Tools and the CipherTrace shutdown

A small number of specialist firms dominate blockchain analytics. Chainalysis (its Reactor investigation tool and KYT transaction-monitoring product) is the market leader; Elliptic (UK-based) and TRM Labs are the other principal providers. All three remain active and are widely used by law enforcement, regulators, and financial institutions.[1]

The most notable recent departure from the field is CipherTrace. Acquired by Mastercard in 2021, CipherTrace's key products — Armada, Inspector, and Sentry — were shut down by Mastercard in March 2024 after the underlying data was reportedly characterized internally as "unverifiable" and "unauditable," effectively ending the product line and leaving Chainalysis and its peers to consolidate the market.[2] The episode is a caution that blockchain-analytics conclusions are only as reliable as the labeling and attribution data behind them, and that vendor claims warrant the same scrutiny an examiner would apply to any expert method under evidentiary standards.

3 Privacy coins, mixers and chain-hopping

Launderers use several techniques to defeat tracing:

  • Privacy coins — cryptocurrencies engineered to obscure transaction details. Monero (XMR) conceals sender, receiver, and amount by default using ring signatures, stealth addresses, and confidential transactions, making conventional clustering largely ineffective. Zcash (ZEC) offers optional shielded transactions using zero-knowledge proofs, though many Zcash transfers are transparent in practice.
  • Mixers / tumblers — services that pool many users' funds and redistribute them to break the link between input and output addresses. Some are custodial services; others are non-custodial smart contracts. Mixers are a primary layering tool and have themselves been targets of sanctions and prosecution.
  • Chain-hopping — rapidly converting funds across different blockchains and assets (for example Bitcoin → a stablecoin → Monero → back) to force investigators to follow the trail across multiple ledgers, each requiring different tooling and often crossing a privacy coin that severs the chain.

These map directly onto the layering stage of money laundering, and defeating them relies on catching errors — a reused address, a KYC on-ramp, a timing correlation — rather than on brute-force ledger analysis alone.

Privacy technology has produced an ongoing arms race. Against mixers, investigators use timing and amount analysis to correlate deposits with withdrawals, and sanctions and prosecutions have driven several major mixing services offline. Against privacy coins, direct on-chain tracing is often impossible, so investigators concentrate on the boundaries — the moments when value converts from a transparent asset into Monero and back, or when a privacy coin is deposited to or withdrawn from a KYC exchange. Stablecoins add a further wrinkle: because many are issued by centralized entities capable of freezing addresses, funds that pass through them are exposed to a form of intervention that fully decentralized assets are not. The practical lesson for examiners is that few schemes are perfectly opaque end to end; the trail typically survives at the seams between systems, and patience at those seams, not raw computing power, is what recovers the thread.

4 FTX, Binance and enforcement relevance

High-profile cases have made crypto tracing central to modern financial-crime enforcement. The 2022 collapse of the exchange FTX — a multi-billion-dollar fraud involving the misuse of customer funds — turned on reconstructing flows between FTX and its affiliated trading firm, exactly the kind of entity-to-entity mapping blockchain analytics supports, and the ability to trace on-chain movements aided both the criminal case and asset recovery for creditors. Binance, the largest exchange, entered a landmark U.S. settlement in 2023 over Bank Secrecy Act and sanctions failures, underscoring that exchanges are the regulated choke points on which tracing depends and that AML compliance at the on/off-ramp is now vigorously enforced.[3] Together these cases illustrate the field's dual role: tracing serves both to build criminal and regulatory cases and to recover assets for victims, in tandem with the AML regime and cross-border tools described under asset tracing. They also mark a shift in perception: early assumptions that cryptocurrency was inherently untraceable have given way to a record of high-profile recoveries — funds clawed back from ransomware operators, darknet markets, and collapsed exchanges — that has made blockchain analytics a routine investigative expectation rather than a novelty. Each enforcement success, however, pushes sophisticated actors toward privacy coins and non-custodial tools, keeping the tracing field in continuous evolution alongside the obfuscation methods it is built to defeat. (Case-specific details here are drawn from general public knowledge and are noted as secondary where not independently sourced in the project briefs.)

See also: Money Laundering, Asset Tracing, OSINT Tools, AML Law.

References

  1. Chainalysis (Reactor / KYT), Elliptic, TRM Labs — active blockchain-analytics providers. (project brief on crypto tracing)
  2. Fortune. "Mastercard shuts down CipherTrace products." March 13, 2024. fortune.com
  3. FTX collapse (2022) and Binance BSA/sanctions settlement (2023). (general public knowledge; secondary)

Anti-Money-Laundering Law

Anti-Money-Laundering Law
Foundational US statuteBank Secrecy Act (1970)
RegulatorFinCEN (U.S. Treasury)
International standard-setterFATF (est. 1989)
Major 2026 changeFinCEN final rule ending US-company BOI reporting
RelatedMoney Laundering, Asset Tracing

Anti-money-laundering (AML) law is the framework of statutes, regulations and international standards designed to detect, deter and punish the laundering of criminal proceeds and the financing of terrorism. It works chiefly by imposing obligations on financial institutions and other businesses — customer identification, record-keeping, and the reporting of cash and suspicious transactions — and by criminalising the movement and concealment of illicit funds. AML law is the legal engine behind much of the practical work described in Money Laundering, Asset Tracing and Cryptocurrency Tracing.[1]

Money laundering is conventionally described in three stages — placement (introducing illicit cash into the financial system), layering (moving it through complex transactions to obscure its origin), and integration (returning it to the criminal as apparently legitimate wealth). This model, derived from FATF and endorsed by bodies such as FinCEN and the UNODC, frames both how laundering works and where the law intervenes.[2]

1 The Bank Secrecy Act

The Bank Secrecy Act (BSA), enacted in 1970, is the foundational U.S. AML statute. It requires financial institutions to keep records and file reports that have a "high degree of usefulness" in criminal, tax and regulatory investigations, and it established the recordkeeping and reporting regime on which all later U.S. AML law builds. The BSA is administered by the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN), a bureau of the U.S. Department of the Treasury. Related early legislation, the Money Laundering Control Act of 1986, made money laundering itself a federal crime.[1]

2 CTRs and SARs

Two reporting obligations sit at the heart of the BSA regime:

  • The Currency Transaction Report (CTR) — a financial institution must file a CTR (FinCEN Form 112) for any cash transaction, or series of cash transactions, exceeding $10,000 in a single business day. The report must generally be filed within 15 days and the records retained for five years.[3]
  • The Suspicious Activity Report (SAR) — institutions must report transactions that appear suspicious regardless of amount, including structuring: breaking a large transaction into smaller pieces specifically to stay under the $10,000 CTR threshold. Structuring is itself a crime, and SARs are a primary intelligence source for investigators.
ReportTriggerThresholdKey detail
CTR (Form 112)Cash transaction(s)> $10,000 / business dayFile within 15 days; retain 5 years
SARSuspicious activityNo dollar thresholdCovers structuring and other red flags

The deliberate splitting of transactions to evade the CTR threshold is one of the classic red flags and a recurring feature of the placement stage of laundering.

3 FATF and the 40 Recommendations

The Financial Action Task Force (FATF), established in 1989, is the intergovernmental body that sets the global AML and counter-terrorist-financing (CFT) standard. Its 40 Recommendations — first issued in 1990, comprehensively revised in 2012, and amended through 2025 (including February 2025 changes to Recommendation 1 aimed at financial inclusion) — constitute the internationally recognised benchmark against which national AML regimes are assessed. Countries are evaluated for compliance and effectiveness, and those with strategic deficiencies may be placed on FATF's monitoring lists, with real consequences for their access to the global financial system.[2]

Because FATF standards drive national law across jurisdictions, they underpin the customer-due-diligence, beneficial-ownership and reporting obligations that investigators encounter worldwide, and they inform cross-border asset tracing.

4 AMLA 2020

The Anti-Money Laundering Act of 2020 (AMLA), enacted as part of the FY2021 National Defense Authorization Act and effective January 1, 2021, was the most significant overhaul of U.S. AML law in decades. Among its many provisions, AMLA created a whistleblower program offering awards of up to 30 percent of monetary sanctions collected in successful enforcement actions, mirroring the incentive structure of the SEC whistleblower regime and materially strengthening the government's ability to surface AML violations. AMLA also expanded FinCEN's authorities and set in motion the beneficial-ownership reporting regime later implemented under the Corporate Transparency Act.[1]

5 Corporate Transparency Act and the 2026 rule

The Corporate Transparency Act (CTA) was enacted to combat the use of anonymous shell companies by requiring many entities to report their beneficial ownership information (BOI) — the real human owners behind a company — to FinCEN. The CTA's implementation, however, was turbulent, marked by litigation over its constitutionality and a series of shifting deadlines and interim rules.[4]

The turning point came in 2026. Following that litigation and an interim final rule issued March 26, 2025, FinCEN adopted a final rule on August 11, 2026 (effective August 14, 2026) that permanently removes the BOI reporting requirement for all U.S. companies and U.S. persons. Under the final rule, only foreign entities registered to do business in the United States remain "reporting companies," and even then they report only their beneficial owners who are non-U.S. persons. FinCEN further indicated it would delete previously collected U.S.-person BOI. The practical effect is that the domestic beneficial-ownership registry, once expected to cover tens of millions of U.S. entities, no longer applies to them.[4]

This is a major and recent change. Older guidance describing broad BOI reporting duties for U.S. LLCs and corporations no longer reflects the law as of August 14, 2026; the reporting obligation now reaches only certain foreign-registered entities. Investigators should treat the domestic BOI registry as effectively wound down for U.S. companies.
MilestoneDateEffect
CTA enacted2021 (via AMLA/NDAA)Created BOI reporting obligation
Interim final ruleMar 26, 2025Narrowed scope amid litigation
FinCEN final ruleAug 11, 2026 (eff. Aug 14, 2026)Ends BOI reporting for US companies/persons; only foreign entities remain

6 Related statutes

AML enforcement rarely stands alone; investigators and prosecutors draw on a wider arsenal of financial-crime and integrity statutes, several of which overlap with money-laundering conduct.[5]

StatuteYearCore content
False Claims Act1863Liability for defrauding the government; qui tam suits let whistleblowers sue on the government's behalf and share in recoveries
Bank Secrecy Act1970Recordkeeping and reporting (CTR/SAR) foundation of US AML
RICO1970Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act; targets patterns of racketeering by criminal enterprises
FCPA1977Foreign Corrupt Practices Act; prohibits bribery of foreign officials and mandates accurate books, records and internal controls
CFAA1986Computer Fraud and Abuse Act; criminalises unauthorised access to computers
SOX2002Sarbanes–Oxley Act; CEO/CFO certification of financial statements and internal-control requirements after Enron and WorldCom
UK Bribery Act2010Four offences including bribing, being bribed, bribing a foreign official, and corporate failure to prevent bribery

Several of these connect directly to other topics in this wiki: SOX and the FCPA's books-and-records provisions bear on financial statement fraud; the FCPA and UK Bribery Act are central to anti-corruption investigation; and the False Claims Act's qui tam mechanism, like AMLA's bounty, shows how whistleblower incentives have become a recurring tool of financial-crime enforcement. RICO's reach over "patterns of racketeering" frequently sweeps in money-laundering counts, tying the AML regime back into broader organised-crime prosecution.

Because AML obligations turn on documented facts — reports filed, records kept, ownership disclosed — enforcement depends heavily on the evidentiary discipline covered in Evidence and Testimony and on preserving records under a sound chain of custody. For the underlying mechanics of how illicit funds move, see Money Laundering.

See also: Money Laundering, Asset Tracing, Cryptocurrency Tracing, Anti-Corruption OSINT.

Open-Source Intelligence (OSINT)

Open-source intelligence (OSINT) is the systematic collection, processing and analysis of information that is freely and publicly available, carried out to answer a specific investigative or intelligence question. The defining characteristic of OSINT is not that the information is free in the sense of costless, nor that it is trivial to obtain, but that it is open: it can be lawfully accessed by a member of the public without recourse to clandestine methods, coercion, or privileged access. In the context of fraud examination and financial-crime investigation, OSINT supplies the raw material — corporate registrations, court filings, property records, social-media traces, leaked datasets, blockchain ledgers — from which analysts extract the patterns that turn scattered facts into evidence.[1]

Open-Source Intelligence
TypeIntelligence discipline
Source basisPublicly and lawfully available information
ProcessGoverned by the intelligence cycle
Sub-disciplinesSOCMINT, GEOINT, HUMINT, IMINT — see OSINT disciplines
Applied inFraud, due diligence, anti-corruption, law enforcement

OSINT is best understood as one discipline within a wider intelligence framework rather than as a standalone activity. It is governed by the same structured workflow — the intelligence cycle — that governs classified collection, and it draws on a recognised catalogue of tools and sub-disciplines. Because open sources touch on real people and their personal data, the practice is also constrained by a body of ethical and legal norms that distinguish legitimate research from unlawful intrusion.

1 Definition and scope

The word "open" in open-source intelligence refers to the accessibility of the source, not to any particular medium or subject matter. Open sources include the obvious — news reporting, government gazettes, company filings, academic literature, patents, and social media — but also the less obvious: satellite imagery, flight and vessel tracking, domain-registration records, freedom-of-information disclosures, and the metadata embedded in photographs and documents. A recurring caution among practitioners is that "freely available does not mean free." Much high-value public data sits behind paywalls, subscription registries, or commercial data brokers, and even nominally free data carries a cost in analyst time, tooling, and the effort of verification.[1]

Equally important is the distinction between information and intelligence. Raw open data — a list of company directors, a cache of tweets, a spreadsheet of transactions — is merely information until it has been evaluated, corroborated and placed in context. The often-repeated maxim that "information is not power — extracting patterns from information is power" captures the analyst's real contribution: the value lies not in having the data but in structuring it, connecting entities, and surfacing the relationship or anomaly that answers the question. This transformation is precisely what the intelligence cycle formalises.

2 Surface, deep and dark web

Analysts commonly divide the web into three layers, each with different access characteristics and evidentiary implications.

LayerDescriptionOSINT relevance
Surface webPages indexed by conventional search engines and reachable by ordinary browsing.News, company websites, indexed social media, blogs; the starting point for most enquiries.
Deep webContent not indexed by search engines — material behind logins, query forms, paywalls or database front-ends. The great majority of online content by volume.Court dockets, corporate and property registries, academic databases, subscription tools; lawful but often requires knowing where to look and how to query.
Dark webNetworks such as Tor requiring specialised software to reach; deliberately obscured hosting.Illicit marketplaces, leaked-credential dumps, ransomware sites; a source of intelligence on fraud and trafficking but raising acute legal and ethical questions.

The deep web is frequently confused with the dark web. In fact the deep web is enormous and largely benign — it simply describes anything not indexed by a search engine, including most of the authoritative registries an investigator relies upon. The dark web is a small, deliberately concealed subset. Accessing dark-web material can shade from passive observation into active reconnaissance, and handling leaked or stolen data raises questions of admissibility and lawfulness that must be resolved before, not after, collection.

3 Role in financial-crime investigation

In fraud and financial-crime work, OSINT rarely proves a case on its own; instead it develops leads, corroborates other evidence, and reveals the connections that a purely document-driven review would miss. A forensic accountant reconstructing a suspect's finances under the net-worth method may use property and corporate registries to identify undisclosed assets; an examiner testing for occupational fraud may use social media to detect lifestyle inconsistent with declared income — a classic behavioural red flag.

OSINT is central to asset tracing and to money-laundering investigations, where the object is to follow value through layers of shell companies and nominees. The rise of digital assets has extended this work onto public blockchains, where cryptocurrency tracing treats the ledger itself as an open source. Techniques such as Benford's Law and other analytical methods are frequently applied to open datasets to flag anomalies worth deeper scrutiny.

4 Due diligence and anti-corruption

Beyond active fraud enquiries, OSINT underpins routine due diligence: know-your-customer checks, adverse-media screening, and the vetting of counterparties, suppliers and prospective hires. Screening a name against sanctions lists, politically-exposed-person (PEP) databases and negative news is a standard first-line control under anti-money-laundering law. The Basel Institute on Governance operates Basel Open Intelligence, a free platform that screens a name against more than 200 financial-crime keywords together with PEP and sanctions data, illustrating how open-source screening has been institutionalised in the compliance sector.[2]

In anti-corruption work the same methods expose conflicts of interest, hidden beneficial ownership and unexplained wealth among public officials. Investigative journalists and civil-society organisations have made OSINT a principal instrument of accountability, cross-referencing leaks, registries and imagery to document graft. These uses are explored further in anti-corruption OSINT.

5 Law-enforcement triggers

For law enforcement, OSINT frequently serves as a trigger — the initial signal that justifies opening or escalating an investigation — rather than as proof in itself. A pattern surfaced from open data may establish the reasonable grounds needed to obtain a production order, subpoena or warrant, at which point the enquiry moves into non-public records. In human-trafficking cases, for example, open social-media and advertisement data can identify victims and networks that financial investigation then corroborates through the money trail, a fusion documented in intergovernmental guidance such as the OSCE compendium Following the Money.[3] The interplay between an OSINT lead and the formal investigative powers it unlocks is one reason the discipline is treated as an entry point to, not a substitute for, conventional evidence gathering. See human-trafficking finance and evidence and testimony.

6 Core principles and limitations

Several recurring principles shape competent OSINT practice:

  • The verification burden. Open data is abundant but unreliable; anyone can publish anything. Every finding must be corroborated against an independent source before it is relied upon. Screenshots, timestamps and archival copies (for example via the Wayback Machine) preserve evidence that may otherwise be edited or deleted, supporting a defensible chain of custody.
  • Infoxication. The volume of available information can overwhelm the analyst — a condition sometimes called information intoxication or infoxication — in which the sheer quantity of data degrades rather than improves judgement. Disciplined scoping under the intelligence cycle is the standard countermeasure.
  • Tools assist, they do not replace judgement. Automated collectors and link-analysis platforms accelerate discovery but cannot evaluate meaning, weigh reliability, or draw the inference; the analyst remains responsible for the conclusion. See OSINT tools.
  • Legality and ethics. Public availability does not confer unlimited licence. Data-protection law, platform terms of service, and the line between passive and active reconnaissance constrain what may be collected and how — the subject of OSINT ethics.

These limitations explain why OSINT, for all its power, is described as an investigative aid. It multiplies what a skilled investigator can find, and it frequently opens doors that would otherwise stay closed, but the analytical work of extracting patterns, testing them and rendering a defensible conclusion remains irreducibly human.

A further consequence of these principles is that OSINT is inherently iterative. A first pass over open sources rarely answers the question; instead it produces new identifiers — an email address, a company number, a username, a wallet — each of which becomes a fresh seed for the next round of collection. This snowball of leads is powerful but also the point at which infoxication most threatens the enquiry, and it is one reason the discipline binds collection to the structured phases of the intelligence cycle rather than allowing it to sprawl. Competent practice is therefore as much about deciding what not to collect, and when to stop, as about finding more.

Finally, OSINT sits at the meeting point of several established fields rather than replacing any of them. It feeds forensic accounting with external corroboration, supplies digital-forensics examiners with context for the artefacts they recover, and provides the leads that interviewing and formal evidence-gathering then convert into testimony. Understood in this way, open-source intelligence is less a separate silo than a connective tissue running through the whole investigative process — the reason a wiki of fraud and financial-crime methods treats it as a hub linking to nearly every other technique.

Historically, the label "open-source intelligence" long predates the internet: intelligence services have exploited newspapers, broadcasts, public records and academic literature for over a century, and the term was formalised in that pre-digital sense. What has changed is scale and reach. The digitisation of registries, the ubiquity of social media, the publication of satellite imagery, and the emergence of public blockchains have expanded the open-source domain enormously, to the point where an investigator sitting at a desk can now assemble a picture that once required extensive fieldwork. That abundance is the discipline's great strength and its central hazard — the same expansion that makes so much discoverable also makes disciplined scoping, verification and ethics indispensable rather than optional.

7 See also

See also: Intelligence Cycle, OSINT Tools, OSINT Disciplines, OSINT Ethics, Cryptocurrency Tracing, Asset Tracing, Anti-Corruption OSINT.
